# 1. NEURAXIAL ANESTHESIA FUNDAMENTALS

## 1.1. OVERVIEW OF TECHNIQUES

### 1.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Techniques of neuraxial anesthesia, clinical applications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in performing neuraxial procedures [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High (Core procedural skill)

---

**Neuraxial anesthesia encompasses a range of techniques that deliver local anesthetics into or around the spinal cord to block nerve conduction.**

* **Neuraxial anesthesia**: General term including subarachnoid (spinal) and epidural injections [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Subarachnoid/intrathecal anesthesia**: Commonly called spinal anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Epidural anesthesia**: Sometimes called "peridural" anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Caudal block**: Injection into the caudal epidural space via the sacral hiatus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Neuraxial anesthesia techniques provide a spectrum of options for anesthetizing the lower half of the body, with variations in technique determining the onset, duration, and flexibility of the block.

## 1.2. SUBARACHNOID (SPINAL) ANESTHESIA CHARACTERISTICS
### 1.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Spinal anesthesia technique and characteristics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in performing spinal anesthesia [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High (Core procedural skill)

---

**Injection of a small dose of local anesthetic directly into the cerebrospinal fluid to achieve rapid, dense surgical anesthesia.**

* **Small dose** of local anesthetic → rapid, dense surgical anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Administration site**: Lumbar region (below spinal cord termination) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Ideal for**: Lower abdominal, pelvic, lower extremity surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Duration limitations**: Single-injection typically 2-3 hours maximum [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* ⚠️ **Not suitable** for prolonged/unpredictable surgeries unless using continuous technique [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Spinal anesthesia provides rapid, dense surgical anesthesia with a small drug dose but has limited duration when performed as a single-shot technique.

## 1.3. CONTINUOUS SUBARACHNOID ANESTHESIA
### 1.3.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Advanced neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Knowledge of specialized techniques and their risks [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Placement of a catheter in the subarachnoid space to allow repeated or continuous administration of local anesthetic.**

* *Mechanism*: Catheter placed in subarachnoid space for extended block duration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Advantages**:
	* Allows careful titration of medication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Better hemodynamic stability in fragile patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Can be redosed during prolonged surgeries [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Disadvantages**:
	* Requires larger gauge needle → higher Post-Dural Puncture Headache (PDPH) risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Risk of misidentification as epidural catheter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Dangerous high block risk** if epidural dose accidentally given via subarachnoid catheter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
* **Historical note**: Microsubarachnoid catheters no longer available due to association with permanent neurologic injury [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Continuous spinal anesthesia offers advantages for titration and extended duration but carries higher risks of PDPH and potentially dangerous dosing errors if the catheter is misidentified.

## 1.4. EPIDURAL ANESTHESIA CHARACTERISTICS
### 1.4.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Epidural anesthesia technique and characteristics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in epidural placement [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High (Core procedural skill)

---

**Injection of local anesthetic into the epidural space, with slower onset but longer potential duration than spinal anesthesia.**

* **Requires larger** local anesthetic doses than spinal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Slower onset** of anesthesia compared to spinal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Advantages**:
	* Continuous anesthesia via catheter for prolonged surgeries [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Can be safely performed at lumbar, thoracic, and cervical levels [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Flexibility for surgical anesthesia or postoperative analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Applications by level**:
	* Lumbar: Similar indications as spinal anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Thoracic: Adjunct to general anesthesia for upper abdominal/thoracic surgeries; postoperative analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Cervical: Rarely used for surgery; mainly for cervical disc pain management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Epidural anesthesia offers greater flexibility in terms of duration and level of placement compared to spinal anesthesia, making it suitable for a wider range of clinical applications including prolonged surgery and postoperative pain management.

## 1.5. CAUDAL ANESTHESIA
### 1.5.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Specialized neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in caudal anesthesia [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Administration of local anesthetic into the epidural space via the sacral hiatus, particularly useful in pediatric patients.**

* **Lower extension** of epidural space accessed via sacral hiatus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Less common** in adults [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Useful for** pediatric surgeries (perineal, lower extremity) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Sacral hiatus anatomy**: Formed by failure of fusion of S5 laminae, bounded above by fused laminae of S4, laterally by margins of deficient laminae of S5 bearing sacral cornua, and inferiorly by posterior surface of S5 body [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Location**: Usually lies 5 cm above the tip of the coccyx, directly beneath the uppermost limit of the natal cleft [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Covered by**: Posterior sacrococcygeal ligament (1-3 mm thick), subcutaneous fat, and skin [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Dural sac termination**: At level of S2 vertebra, allowing safe access to epidural space below this level via sacral hiatus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

**Bottom Line:** Caudal anesthesia provides access to the epidural space via the sacral hiatus and is particularly valuable in pediatric practice for lower body procedures; in adults, the distance from sacral hiatus to dural sac varies considerably (1.5-7.5 cm), requiring careful needle advancement to avoid dural puncture.

## 1.6. EPIDURAL ANALGESIA APPLICATIONS
### 1.6.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Clinical applications of epidural analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C4 Trauma Resuscitation and Crisis Management|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Perioperative pain management competencies [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Use of epidural techniques primarily for pain management rather than surgical anesthesia in various clinical contexts.**

* **Postoperative pain**: Dilute local anesthetic + opioid mixtures → analgesia with minimal motor block [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Key component** in Enhanced Recovery After Surgery (ERAS) protocols [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C4 Trauma Resuscitation and Crisis Management|ⓘ]].
* **Labor analgesia**:
	* Excellent choice due to flexibility of continuous technique [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Dilute solutions → minimal maternal motor block [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Negligible effects on labor progress and outcome [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Can be converted to surgical anesthesia if cesarean delivery needed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Epidural analgesia offers excellent pain relief with minimal motor block, making it ideal for postoperative pain management, ERAS protocols, and labor analgesia.

## 1.7. COMBINED SPINAL-EPIDURAL (CSE) TECHNIQUE
### 1.7.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Advanced neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in CSE technique [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium-High

---

**A technique combining the rapid onset of spinal anesthesia with the flexibility of epidural anesthesia.**

* **Combines rapid onset** of dense spinal anesthesia with flexibility of epidural catheter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Allows initial surgical anesthesia via spinal component with ability to extend duration via epidural [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Particularly useful for procedures of uncertain duration or when postoperative analgesia will be needed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Combined spinal-epidural technique provides the best features of both spinal (rapid onset, dense block) and epidural (extendable duration, postoperative analgesia) approaches.

# 2. INDICATIONS AND OUTCOMES

## 2.1. INDICATIONS
### 2.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Clinical applications of neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Decision-making regarding appropriate anesthetic technique [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Neuraxial techniques can be appropriately applied to a range of surgical procedures based on clinical context and patient factors.**

* **No absolute indications** for neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Determined by** combination of patient, surgeon, and anesthesiologist preferences and clinical context [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Useful for**: Lower abdominal, inguinal, urogenital, rectal, lower extremity surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Lumbar spinal surgery** may also be performed under spinal anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Upper abdominal procedures**: Less common due to difficulty achieving adequate sensory level safely [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** While there are no absolute indications for neuraxial anesthesia, it is particularly well-suited for lower abdominal, pelvic, and lower extremity procedures, with technique selection based on clinical context and patient/provider preferences.

## 2.2. CONTRAINDICATIONS
### 2.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Safety considerations and contraindications for neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and risk assessment [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Conditions that either absolutely prohibit or relatively increase the risk of neuraxial anesthesia.**

* **Absolute Contraindications**:
	* Patient refusal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
	* Infection at the injection site [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Severe uncorrected hypovolemia / Hemodynamic instability [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Increased intracranial pressure (ICP), particularly with focal neurologic signs (risk of brain herniation) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* True allergy to local anesthetics (rare) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Relative Contraindications**:
	* Coagulopathy or therapeutic anticoagulation (Requires careful assessment - See ANTICOAGULATION section) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Sepsis or bacteremia (risk of epidural abscess) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Preexisting neurological disease (e.g., multiple sclerosis - controversial, requires discussion) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Severe spinal deformity (e.g., kyphoscoliosis) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Uncooperative patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Fixed cardiac output states (e.g., severe aortic stenosis - risk of profound hypotension) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Absolute contraindications to neuraxial anesthesia include patient refusal, local infection, severe hemodynamic instability, increased ICP, and LA allergy; relative contraindications require careful risk-benefit assessment and potential modification of technique or approach.

## 2.3. OUTCOMES AND EVIDENCE
### 2.3.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Evidence-based practice in neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Integration of evidence into clinical decision-making [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#5. Practice-Based Learning and Improvement|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium-High

---

**Research findings regarding the impact of neuraxial anesthesia on various clinical outcomes.**

### 2.3.2. MORTALITY AND MORBIDITY DATA
* 🔑 **HY**: Subarachnoid and epidural anesthesia associated with:
	* Lower 30-day mortality risk (Risk Ratio 0.71) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Reduced postoperative pneumonia risk (Risk Ratio 0.45) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* No reduction in myocardial infarction risk (Risk Ratio 1.17) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Combined neuraxial + general anesthesia vs. general alone**:
	* **No reduction** in death or MI risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Reduced pneumonia risk** (Risk Ratio 0.69) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 2.3.3. SURGICAL TYPE-SPECIFIC OUTCOMES
* **Hip fracture surgery**:
	* Initial retrospective studies suggested decreased mortality/pulmonary complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Larger follow-up retrospective study: No mortality difference [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Recent randomized trial (REGAIN): No improvement in 60-day survival or ambulation; similar delirium incidence vs GA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* 🏥 **Clinical Practice**: Choice often based on patient factors, surgeon preference, and potential for faster recovery/less opioid use, rather than proven mortality benefit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
* **Hip/knee replacement**:
	* Retrospective data suggests lower 30-day mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Fewer prolonged hospital stays [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Lower extremity amputation**:
	* Fewer postoperative pulmonary complications and arrhythmias [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Shorter ICU stays [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **COPD patients**:
	* Fewer adverse respiratory events (pneumonia, ventilator dependence, unplanned intubation) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Total knee arthroplasty**:
	* Fewer wound infections, blood transfusions, pneumonias, total infections [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Shorter hospital stays [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Improved outcomes predicting discharge readiness (pain control, PONV reduction) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Potential healthcare cost reduction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Contradictory finding**: One study found increased superficial wound/UTIs with spinal for hip fracture [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 2.3.4. THROMBOEMBOLISM RISK
* **Some studies** show lower VTE/PE risk with neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Proposed mechanism*: Reduced hypercoagulable state, increased tissue blood flow [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Not consistently confirmed** across all studies [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Insufficient data** for major vascular surgery benefit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **No improvement** in graft patency after lower extremity revascularization [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 2.3.5. CANCER OUTCOMES
* **Early meta-analysis**: Epidural anesthesia/analgesia associated with longer survival [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Proposed mechanism*: Avoidance of immunosuppression from general anesthesia and opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Current status**: Recent data on cancer recurrence less promising; benefit remains controversial [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Potential benefit may be discussed for oncologic surgeries, but evidence is not definitive [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

### 2.3.6. OTHER POTENTIAL BENEFITS
* **Reduced bleeding** and transfusion requirements (some studies) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Earlier return** of GIT function (reduced ileus) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Mechanism*: Reduced systemic opioids, enhanced peristalsis (vagal dominance) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Suppression** of the neuroendocrine stress response to surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* More complete for lower extremity surgery vs. major abdominal/thoracic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** While neuraxial anesthesia shows benefits in reducing pneumonia and possibly mortality in some patient populations, the outcomes vary by surgical type and patient factors. Recent high-quality evidence shows less dramatic benefits than earlier retrospective studies, suggesting careful individualized assessment is needed.

# 3. VERTEBRAL ANATOMY

## 3.1. VERTEBRAL COLUMN OVERVIEW
### 3.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Anatomy of the vertebral column and spinal canal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Anatomical considerations for neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Application of anatomical knowledge to procedure performance [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**The vertebral column houses and protects the spinal cord while providing structural support for the body.**

* **Vertebral column**: 33 vertebrae (7 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, 5 fused sacral, 4 fused coccygeal) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Structure purpose**: Protects vertebral canal containing spinal cord, CSF, meninges, nerves, epidural space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Vertebral components**:
	* **Body**: Anterior portion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Pedicles**: Project posteriorly from body [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Laminae**: Connect pedicles posteriorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Transverse processes**: Arise laterally from pedicle-lamina junction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Spinous process**: Projects posteriorly from laminae union [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Vertebral notches (Superior/Inferior)**: Form intervertebral foramina for nerve exit [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Articular processes (Superior/Inferior)**: Form facet joints between vertebrae [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].

	![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image.png_843c1c.png_29f163.png_e94ae6.png)

* **Spinal Canal**: Formed by stacked vertebral rings [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Intervertebral Disks**: Connect vertebral bodies [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Spinal curvatures**: Four curvatures present in the adult spine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] 
	* Cervical and lumbar curvatures are convex forward (lordosis)
	* Thoracic and sacral curvatures are concave forward (kyphosis)
	* Cervical compensatory curve develops when infant holds up head, lumbar curve follows when child sits and stands

**Bottom Line:** The vertebral column consists of 33 vertebrae forming a protective canal housing the spinal cord and its associated structures, with each vertebra having characteristic components that are important to understand for neuraxial anesthesia.

## 3.2. SPECIALIZED VERTEBRAE
### 3.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Specific vertebral anatomy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Anatomical landmarks for neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Different regions of the vertebral column have specialized vertebrae with unique characteristics.**

* **Cervical Vertebrae (C1-C2)**:
	* **C1 (Atlas)**: Lacks vertebral body and spinous process; unique skull articulation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **C2 (Axis)**: Has dens (odontoid process) for articulation with C1 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **C7 (Vertebra Prominens)**: Strong, non-bifid spine that is first clearly palpable when running fingers down nuchal furrow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Thoracic Vertebrae (T1-T12)**: Articulate with corresponding ribs. Spinous processes slant caudally [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **T1 spine**: Most readily palpable of vertebral spines, projects almost horizontally backward [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Mid-thoracic spines**: Angled caudally, requiring marked cephalad needle angulation for midline approach [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **T11-T12 spines**: Return to horizontal orientation, short and square like lumbar type [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Lumbar Vertebrae (L1-L5)**:
	* Large bodies; spinous processes more horizontal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* Vertebral foramina roughly triangular [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* Laminae short, broad, and strong, but do not overlap each other as in thoracic region [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **L5**: Wedge-shaped (deeper in front than behind) creating lumbosacral angle; transverse processes thick and strong, arising from both arch and vertebral body [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Sacrum**:
	* **5 fused vertebrae** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Sacralization**: Fusion of L5-S1 (0.9-6.4% of population) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Hemisacralization**: Unilateral fusion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Sacral hiatus**: Caudal opening due to non-fusion of S4/S5 laminae; access point for caudal block [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
		* Always patent in children, variable patency in adults [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Coccyx**: 4 fused vertebrae; anchor for ligaments/muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Each region of the spine has distinctive vertebral characteristics, with key variations in cervical, thoracic, and lumbar regions that influence approaches to neuraxial anesthesia, while sacral anatomy is particularly important for caudal techniques.

## 3.3. CLINICAL SURFACE LANDMARKS
### 3.3.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Surface anatomy relevant to neuraxial procedures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Practical application of landmarks for neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical skill in identifying appropriate landmarks [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Practical surface landmarks help identify appropriate levels for neuraxial procedures.**

* **Key palpable landmarks**:
	* **C7 spinous process** (*vertebra prominens*) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **T7 spinous process**: Inferior angle of scapula (arms at side) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **L1 spinous process**: Often near lower costal margin/elbow level [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **L4 spinous process / L4-L5 interspace**: *Tuffier's line* (line between iliac crests) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* ⚠️ **Often misleading** (can be L3-L4 or L5-S1), especially in obese patients or with positioning changes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **S2 spinous process**: Dimples of Venus / Posterior Superior Iliac Spines (PSIS) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **More reliable approach**: Identify sacral hiatus or L5-S1 interspace and count cephalad [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Obese patients**: Use midline skin crease and gluteal cleft to locate midline [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Ultrasound**: Increasingly used for accurate level identification [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. (See ULTRASOUND ANATOMY section)
* **Intercristal line**: Line joining top of iliac crests usually passes through body of L4 vertebra, useful for identifying L3/4 interspace (above line) and L4/5 interspace (below line) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

**Bottom Line:** Surface landmarks are essential for identifying appropriate levels for neuraxial procedures, though they can be misleading, particularly in obese patients or with positional changes, making ultrasound an increasingly valuable adjunct.

## 3.4. SPINAL LIGAMENTS
### 3.4.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Ligamentous anatomy of the vertebral column [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Structures encountered during neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Sequential ligamentous structures that must be traversed during neuraxial procedures, each with characteristic resistance.**

* **Supraspinous ligament**:
	* **Connects tips** of spinous processes (C7 to sacrum) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Continues as** ligamentum nuchae above C7 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Thinner/absent** in lower lumbar region [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* *Feel*: Dense, fibrous "snap" often felt [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* May become ossified in elderly patients, making midline spinal puncture difficult [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Interspinous ligament**:
	* **Runs between** adjacent spinous processes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **May contain** fat-filled cavities → potential false loss of resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Merges with** supraspinous ligament posteriorly and ligamentum flavum anteriorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* *Feel*: Less dense, "mushy" or variable resistance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Thin and tenuous, particularly in cervical region [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Ligamentum flavum** (*yellow ligament*):
	* **Connects laminae** of adjacent vertebrae [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Dense, elastic** tissue (mostly elastin) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Two ligaments** fuse in midline (variable angle) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Midline gaps** may exist (especially lumbar, ~10% of patients), potentially leading to false LOR or easier paramedian entry [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Feel*: Marked increase in resistance, "gritty" feel, followed by loss of resistance upon entering epidural space [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Progressively thickens from above downwards; elastic recoil transmitted to syringe during epidural placement more obvious in lumbar than thoracic region [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* Elasticity often lost in elderly patients with possible calcification [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Anterior/Posterior longitudinal ligaments**: Link vertebral bodies anteriorly/posteriorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_1.png_33a1ae.png_d2b07e.png_07b502.png)

**Bottom Line:** Understanding the sequence and characteristics of spinal ligaments is crucial for neuraxial procedures, as they provide tactile feedback during needle advancement and help identify entry into the epidural space.

## 3.5. EPIDURAL SPACE
### 3.5.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Anatomy of the epidural space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Anatomical considerations for epidural anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**A potential space outside the dural sac that contains fat, blood vessels, and nerves, and is the target for epidural anesthesia.**

* *Definition*: Potential space within vertebral canal, outside the dural sac [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Anatomical boundaries**:
	* **Superior**: Foramen magnum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Inferior**: Sacrococcygeal membrane (covering sacral hiatus) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Anterior**: Posterior longitudinal ligament [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Posterior**: Lamina and ligamentum flavum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Lateral**: Pedicles and intervertebral foramina (communicates with paravertebral space) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_2.png_df688b.png_242502.png_c47630.png)

* **Key characteristics**:
	* **Potential space**: Contains fat, areolar tissue, lymphatics, veins, nerve roots [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Discontinuous**: Dura may intermittently contact bone/ligaments. Fat often in discrete pockets [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Fat content varies**: Minimal in cervical, more abundant in lumbar (may affect drug kinetics) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Negative pressure**: Can be present (especially thoracic), basis for hanging drop technique. Pressure varies with respiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Segmental structure**: Segmental nature demonstrated using contrast radiography and MRI; wedge-shaped segments especially marked in midline [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Segmental size variations**: MRI studies show lower end of each segment approximately 4 mm wide compared with 6 mm at upper end, though injection of fluids may distort these measurements due to space distensibility [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Fibrous bands**: Bands stretching from dura to ligamentum flavum may divert epidural catheters, though the existence of a plica mediana dorsalis is likely an injection artifact [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Epidural veins (Batson's plexus)**:
	* **Located mostly** in anterior/lateral epidural space. Valveless [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Network arrangement**: Four main trunks running vertically (two along posterior longitudinal ligament anteriorly, two posteriorly in front of vertebral arches) with venous rings connecting at each vertebral level [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Connections**: Receives basivertebral veins emerging from each vertebral body plus communicating branches from vertebral, cervical, intercostal, lumbar, iliolumbar and lateral sacral veins [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Engorgement**: Occurs with increased intra-abdominal pressure (e.g., pregnancy, obesity, coughing) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* → Increases risk of venous cannulation during epidural placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* → May decrease effective epidural space volume, potentially enhancing spread [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* → Provides connecting link between pelvic veins below and cerebral veins above (potential pathway for spreading bacteria/malignant cells) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

**Bottom Line:** The epidural space is a non-continuous potential space containing fat, vessels, and nerves; its characteristics vary by spinal level and physiologic state, affecting both technical aspects of epidural placement and drug distribution.

## 3.6. MENINGES
### 3.6.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Meningeal anatomy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Structures traversed during neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Three protective layers surrounding the spinal cord that are relevant to neuraxial anesthesia.**

* **Three layers** protecting spinal cord, CSF, nerve roots [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]]:
	* Dura mater (outermost) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]]
	* Arachnoid mater (middle) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]]
	* Pia mater (innermost) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]]

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_3.png_c1cde1.png_abc5c6.png_71d7cf.png)

### 3.6.2. DURA MATER ("HARD MOTHER")

* **Structure**: Thickest layer (~270-280 μm), dense collagen/elastin fibers in lamellae [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Anatomical course**:
	* **Extends from** foramen magnum to ~S2 level in adults (S3 in children) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Fuses with** filum terminale inferiorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Extends laterally** with nerve roots, fusing with epineurium near intervertebral foramina [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Subdural space**:
	* *Potential space* between dura and arachnoid [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Rare site** for unintended injection → unpredictable, extensive block ("subdural block") [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Composition**: In the brain, dural covering is a double membrane with cerebral venous sinuses between walls; the dura covering the cord consists of only the inner (meningeal) layer of the cerebral dura [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

### 3.6.3. ARACHNOID MATER ("SPIDER MOTHER")

* **Structure**: Delicate, avascular membrane. Adherent to inner dura [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Compact laminar layer**: Tight junctions form primary barrier to drug movement [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Trabecular portion**: Web-like strands crossing subarachnoid space to pia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* 🔑 **HY**: Arachnoid is the primary barrier regulating drug passage between epidural and subarachnoid spaces [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Keeps CSF** within the subarachnoid space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Arachnoid granulations**: Projections into dural venous sinuses (mainly cranial, some spinal) involved in CSF absorption (traditional view) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].

### 3.6.4. PIA MATER ("SOFT MOTHER")

* **Structure**: Thin, vascular layer adherent to spinal cord and nerve roots [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Contains fenestrations**: Role in drug action speculative [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Forms dentate ligaments**: Anchor cord laterally to dura [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Forms filum terminale**: Anchors cord inferiorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Specialized structures**:
	* **Linea splendens**: Thickened anterior pia along anterior median fissure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Ligamentum denticulatum**: 21 triangular fibrous strands attaching pia to dura between spinal nerves down to T12-L1 gap; lowermost denticulation is bifid and crossed by L1 nerve root [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Posterior subarachnoid septum**: Incomplete sheet from posterior median sulcus to dura in midline [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

**Bottom Line:** The meninges consist of three layers (dura, arachnoid, pia) with the arachnoid being the primary barrier for drug movement between epidural and subarachnoid spaces; understanding these layers is crucial for neuraxial techniques and drug spread.

## 3.7. SUBARACHNOID SPACE AND CSF
### 3.7.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Cerebrospinal fluid anatomy and physiology [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Site of action for spinal anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**The space between the arachnoid and pia maters containing cerebrospinal fluid, which is the site of drug action for spinal anesthesia.**

### 3.7.2. SUBARACHNOID SPACE

* **Location**: Between arachnoid and pia mater [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Contents**: Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), spinal cord, nerve roots, blood vessels [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Site of action** for spinal anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Traversing structures**: Trabeculae including posterior subarachnoid septum and ligamentum denticulatum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Communication**: With tissue spaces around vessels in pia mater that penetrate into the cord, though traditional concept of Virchow-Robin spaces as pathways for spinal anesthetic penetration is debatable and possibly an artifact of histological preparation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

### 3.7.3. CEREBROSPINAL FLUID (CSF)

* **Composition**: Clear, colorless fluid. ~99% water + proteins, glucose, electrolytes, neurotransmitters. Specific gravity ~1.003-1.008 at 37°C [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Volume**: ~100-160 mL total in adults (spinal portion ~30-80 mL). Variable [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Production/Absorption (Updated View)**:
	* **Produced** mainly by choroid plexus AND brain/spinal cord capillaries (CSF-IF exchange) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Absorbed** mainly via brain/spinal cord capillaries and lymphatic system (not just arachnoid granulations) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Choroid plexus**: Highly vascular pia invaginations covered by single-cell ependymal epithelium in lateral, 3rd, and 4th ventricles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **CSF composition**: Varies from true dialysate of plasma; active secretion and selective diffusion by ependymal cells suggested by "blood-brain barrier" effects on many drugs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Flow pattern**:
	* **Escapes 4th ventricle** through median foramen of Magendie and lateral foramina of Luschka into cerebral subarachnoid space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Not unidirectional bulk flow** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Local mixing** driven by cardiac/respiratory pulsations [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Drug spread**: Primarily by bulk displacement initially, then slower diffusion and local mixing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Solute clearance**: Reabsorption across capillary membranes into bloodstream [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **CSF Pressure**:
	* **Normal**: 70-180 mm CSF in lateral position [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Postural changes**: In sitting position, rises to 350-550 mm CSF at lumbar level while ventricular pressure falls below atmospheric [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Increased with**: Coughing, straining, jugular venous compression (Queckenstedt's test) - transmitted via cerebral venous engorgement and epidural venous shunting [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Factors influencing spread**: CSF volume/density, patient position, baricity of solution, injection force/speed. (See PHYSIOLOGY section) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** The subarachnoid space contains CSF, which is continuously produced and absorbed through multiple pathways; understanding CSF dynamics is crucial for predicting drug spread and duration in spinal anesthesia.

## 3.8. SPINAL CORD
### 3.8.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Spinal cord anatomy and neurophysiology [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Neuroanatomical considerations for neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**The main neural structure within the vertebral canal that must be protected during neuraxial anesthesia.**

### 3.8.2. GENERAL ANATOMY
* **Anatomy**: Cylindrical structure extending from medulla oblongata [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Length**: 45 cm in adult (equal to femur length, was deferens, and lips-to-esophagogastric junction distance) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Shape**: Elongated cylinder, somewhat flattened anteroposteriorly, with cervical and lumbar enlargements corresponding to origins of brachial and lumbosacral plexuses [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Termination (Conus Medullaris)**:
	* **First trimester fetus**: Extends to sacrum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **At birth**: Usually ends at L3 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **In adults**: Most often ends at L1 (range T12-L3) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Needle insertion**: Should be below L1 (adults) or L3 (infants) to avoid cord injury [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![Untitled](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_Untitled.png_3328a2.png_9db41b.png_6f36e4.png)

![Untitled](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_Untitled_1.png_4113bf.png_35da6e.png_8c41f0.png)

* **Spinal nerves**:
	* **31 pairs**: 8 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, 5 sacral, 1 coccygeal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Roots**: Anterior (motor) and Posterior (sensory, with dorsal root ganglion) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Dermatome**: Specific skin area innervated by a single posterior sensory root [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].

	![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_4.png_d46ede.png_b69f07.png_db84d2.png)

* **Sympathetic nervous system outflow**:
	* **Origin**: Intermediolateral grey matter (T1-L2). Preganglionic fibers exit with spinal nerves [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Blockade**: Neuraxial anesthesia blocks preganglionic sympathetic fibers → vasodilation, etc. (See PHYSIOLOGY section) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Naming convention**:
	* **Cervical**: Nerve exits ABOVE corresponding vertebra (C8 exits below C7) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Thoracic/Lumbar/Sacral**: Nerve exits BELOW corresponding vertebra [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Cauda equina** ("horse's tail"):
	* **Lumbar and sacral** spinal nerves extending beyond conus medullaris within dural sac [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Covered only by** pia mater (less protected than cord) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Relatively mobile**: Tend to be pushed aside by needle rather than pierced [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **May be more susceptible** to chemical injury (high local anesthetic concentrations) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Neuroanatomical volumes**:
	* **Dural sac volume** (S1-T12): ~43 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **CSF volume**: ~34 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Neural tissue volume** (conus + roots): ~9-10 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Nerve volume ratio**: Increases significantly from L5 (7-14%) towards T12 (30-43%) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Upper lumbar punctures** risk conus medullaris contact [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Lower punctures** risk cauda equina contact (less likely to cause permanent injury than cord) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Spinal cord structure**:
	* **Anterior median fissure** and shallow posterior median sulcus with posterior median septum extending halfway into cord [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Posterolateral sulci** marking emergence of posterior nerve roots [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Central canal** continuing from 4th ventricle, lined with ciliated ependymal cells, contains CSF, traverses whole cord with slight dilation in conus medullaris [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **H-shaped grey matter** surrounded by white matter, with grey matter proportion greater in cervical and lumbar enlargements [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Grey matter components**: Transverse commissure (cross-limb of H), anterior horn (motor), posterior horn (sensory capped by substantia gelatinosa), lateral grey column (T1-L2, sympathetic origin) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **White matter**: Mainly longitudinal myelinated nerve fibers divided into posterior, lateral, and anterior columns [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

### 3.8.3. ASCENDING AND DESCENDING PATHWAYS
* **Ascending pathways** carry sensory information from periphery to brain [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]]:
	* **Dorsal column (posterior funiculus)**: Fine touch, vibration, proprioception. Remains ipsilateral [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Spinothalamic tract (lateral funiculus)**: Pain and temperature. Crosses midline within cord [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Spinocerebellar tracts**: Unconscious proprioception to cerebellum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Descending pathways** carry motor commands from brain to periphery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]]:
	* **Corticospinal tract (lateral funiculus)**: Voluntary motor control. Crosses midline at medulla [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Rubrospinal tract**: Motor coordination [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Reticulospinal tract**: Posture, tone, autonomic function [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].

![nse-2049787206149636173-115013.jpg](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_nse-2049787206149636173-115013.jpg_8e9b2e.jpg_ddbe65.jpg_d6be28.jpg)

![nse-1909240047624712131-111291.jpg](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_nse-1909240047624712131-111291.jpg_004e63.jpg_8ddc70.jpg_c26adf.jpg)

### 3.8.4. SPINAL CORD BLOOD SUPPLY
* **Anterior Spinal Artery (ASA)**:
	* **Origin**: Vertebral arteries [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Supplies**: Anterior 2/3 of the cord [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Variations**: Diameter may vary considerably, may disappear completely for short segments [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Posterior Spinal Arteries (PSA)**:
	* **Origin**: Vertebral or PICA (Posterior Inferior Cerebellar Arteries). Paired [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Supplies**: Posterior 1/3 of the cord [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Radicular Arteries**:
	* **Segmental reinforcement** from intercostal/lumbar arteries [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Number**: Most anterior radicular arteries are small, but 4-9 larger ones (usually in lower cervical, lower thoracic, and upper lumbar regions) reinforce the anterior spinal arteries [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* **Artery of Adamkiewicz** (*arteria radicularis magna*):
		* **Largest radicular artery**. Usually unilateral (left side, T9-T12 origin common) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
		* **Major supply** to lower 2/3 of anterior cord [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
		* **Sends branches** to both anterior and posterior spinal arteries [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
		* ⚠️ **Vulnerable** during thoracic surgery or aortic procedures → risk of anterior spinal artery syndrome (motor paralysis, loss of pain/temp, preserved proprioception/light touch) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Arterial anatomy vulnerability**:
	* Cord blood supply may be jeopardized by:
		* Surgical cross-clamping of thoracic aorta
		* Aortic dissection
		* Vasoconstrictors in epidural anesthesia
		* Deliberate or accidental hypotension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

![Untitled](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_Untitled_2.png_ef82f3.png_0c5930.png_eca9bb.png)

**Bottom Line:** The spinal cord terminates at L1 in adults (range T12-L3), with spinal nerves continuing as the cauda equina; understanding cord anatomy, blood supply, and neural pathways is essential for safe neuraxial anesthesia and recognizing potential complications.

## 3.9. SACRAL ANATOMY
### 3.9.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Anatomy of the sacrum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Anatomical considerations for caudal anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**The sacrum forms the base of the vertebral column and is an important structure for caudal block technique.**

* **Structure**: Triangular shaped bony structure formed from five fused sacral vertebrae [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Surfaces**:
	* **Anterior**: Concave, formed from the fused vertebral bodies [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Posterior**: Convex, with median sacral crest formed from fused spinous processes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Base**: Articulates with L5 vertebra [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* **Lateral**: Articulates with iliac bones via sacroiliac joints [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Sacral canal**: Commences behind the body of S1 and terminates at the sacral hiatus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Sacral hiatus**: Formed by failure of S5 vertebra to fuse in midline, covered by sacrococcygeal membrane [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Borders**:
		* **Superior**: Laminae of S4 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
		* **Lateral**: Laminae of S5/sacral cornua [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
		* **Posterior**: Sacrococcygeal membrane [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
		* **Inferior**: Coccyx and body of S5 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Contents of sacral canal**:
	* Dura and filum terminale (ends at S3 in adults) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* Epidural space (fat, blood vessels) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* Sacral nerve roots [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* Sacral venous plexus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
	* Lymphatics and areolar tissue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Sacrococcygeal connections**:
	* Thin fibrocartilaginous disc between sacrum and coccyx [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* Posterior sacrococcygeal ligament connects cornua, spreads as membrane covering sacral hiatus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* Anterior sacrococcygeal ligament comprising weak longitudinal fibers [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* Lateral sacrococcygeal ligaments connecting inferior lateral angle of sacrum to coccyx transverse process, roofing over S5 nerve emergence [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Sacral hiatus variations**: Classical apex at lower third of S4 in 35% of humans; in 20% it's lower (even to S5), and in 45% it's higher (up to lower third of S2). Rarely, sacral canal may be open throughout [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Other anomalies**:
	* Deficiencies between superior and inferior limits of posterior wall
	* Bilateral gaps on either side of midline
	* Canal obliteration by transverse fold, dorsal projection, or bony overgrowth [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

![Sacral Anatomy.001.jpeg](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_Sacral_Anatomy.001.jpeg_00e9d1.jpeg_f60084.jpeg_9cabf8.jpeg)

**Bottom Line:** The sacrum is a triangular bone forming the base of the spine; the sacral hiatus, formed by unfused S5 laminae, provides access to the epidural space for caudal anesthesia, with the distance from hiatus to dural sac varying from 1.5-7.5 cm.

## 3.10. VERTEBRAL ANOMALIES
### 3.10.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Developmental anomalies of the spine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Impact of anomalies on neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Developmental variations in vertebral anatomy that may impact neuraxial procedures.**

* **Clinical significance**: Vertebral anomalies are not merely academic but may render neuraxial blocks difficult or impossible [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Embryological basis**:
	* Mesodermal somites condense around primitive notochord and neural tube
	* Each vertebral body originates from one half of each of two adjacent somites (intersegmental development)
	* Primary ossification centers develop one on either side of vertebral arch and one within body (occasionally two centers side by side in body) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Vertebral body defects**:
	* Additional vertebrae or hemivertebrae (latter causing congenital scoliosis)
	* Anterior spina bifida (especially cervical and lumbar) from failure of body centers to unite
	* Absence of vertebrae or lower sacrum/coccyx [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Fusion anomalies**:
	* Complete or partial fusion of two or more vertebrae
	* **Sacralization of L5**: Partial or complete fusion of L5 to sacrum
	* **Lumbarization of S1**: Partial or complete separation of S1 from rest of sacrum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Neural arch defects (Spina bifida)**:
	* Failure of fusion of arch centers
	* **Types** (in order of increasing severity):
		1. **Spina bifida occulta**: Failure of vertebral arch fusion only; normal meninges and nervous tissue; may have overlying dimple, lipoma, or tuft of hair
		2. **Meningocele**: Protrusion of meninges through posterior vertebral defect
		3. **Myelomeningocele**: Neural tissue protrudes into and may adhere to meningeal sac
		4. **Myelocele (rachischisis)**: Failure of neural tube fusion with open spinal plate; incompatible with survival
	* Most common in L5 or upper sacral region (6% in L5, 11% in upper two sacral segments) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Spondylolisthesis**:
	* Defect in neural arch of L5 (occasionally L4) allowing forward slip of vertebral body with pedicles and superior articular processes
	* May result from failure of two ossification centers in neural arch to fuse [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

**Bottom Line:** Vertebral anomalies including variations in fusion (sacralization/lumbarization), spina bifida, and spondylolisthesis can significantly affect the performance of neuraxial anesthesia and should be considered when unexpected technical difficulties are encountered.

# 4. ULTRASOUND ANATOMY OF THE SPINE

## 4.1. CLINICAL UTILITY
### 4.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Advanced techniques for neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in ultrasound-assisted neuraxial blocks [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium-High

---

**Ultrasound guidance improves the success rate and safety of neuraxial procedures by allowing visualization of anatomy.**

* **Complements clinical examination**, especially with difficult landmarks (obesity, scoliosis) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Establishes depth** of epidural space [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Identifies precise** intervertebral level and midline [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Locates interspinous/interlaminar** spaces ("acoustic window") [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Increases first-pass success rate, potentially reduces traumatic procedures and complications, even for experienced providers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* 🔑 **HY**: Most experts use pre-procedural ultrasound ("scout scan") rather than real-time guidance for needle insertion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Ultrasound improves neuraxial techniques by identifying precise levels and depths, especially valuable with difficult anatomy, though typically used as a pre-procedural guide rather than for real-time needle visualization.

## 4.2. BASIC ULTRASOUND EXAMINATION TECHNIQUES
### 4.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Sonographic anatomy of the spine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Ultrasound techniques for neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in spinal ultrasonography [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Standard approaches to ultrasound imaging of the spine involve paramedian longitudinal and transverse scanning techniques.**

* **Probe**: Low-frequency (2-5 MHz) curvilinear transducer preferred for deeper structures. High-frequency linear probe for superficial structures/pediatrics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Patient position**: Usually sitting or lateral, same as for block placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 4.2.2. PARAMEDIAN LONGITUDINAL (SAGITTAL) SCAN

* **Probe placement**: 2-3 cm lateral to midline, parallel to spine, tilted slightly medially [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **View**: "Sawtooth" pattern of overlapping laminae. Interlaminar spaces appear as hypoechoic gaps [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Visible structures**:
	* **Sacrum**: Continuous hyperechoic line inferiorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Laminae**: Hyperechoic curved lines (saw teeth) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Articular processes**: Deeper, more lateral bony structures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Ligamentum Flavum/Dura Mater complex**: Hyperechoic line deep to interlaminar space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Posterior Longitudinal Ligament/Vertebral Body complex**: Deeper hyperechoic line (anterior boundary of spinal canal) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Epidural space**: Hypoechoic area between ligamentum flavum/dura and PLL/vertebral body [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Intrathecal space**: Anechoic (dark) area deep to dura, may see nerve roots or cord pulsation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 4.2.3. TRANSVERSE (AXIAL) SCAN

* **Probe placement**: Perpendicular to spine, centered over desired interspace identified in longitudinal view [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **View**: Symmetrical image showing spinous process posteriorly, transverse processes laterally [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Visible structures (midline interspinous view)**:
