	* **Spinous process**: Hyperechoic with posterior shadow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Interspinous ligament**: Hypoechoic between spinous processes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Ligamentum Flavum/Dura Mater complex**: Hyperechoic line deep to ligament [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Epidural space**: Hypoechoic area deep to ligamentum flavum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Intrathecal space**: Anechoic area deep to dura [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Posterior Longitudinal Ligament/Vertebral Body complex**: Deepest hyperechoic line [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Visible structures (transverse interlaminar view - probe slightly off midline)**:
	* **Laminae**: Hyperechoic curved structures laterally [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Ligamentum Flavum/Dura**: Hyperechoic line spanning between laminae [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![US.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_US.png_7ed65f.png_0b14a0.png_3a975d.png)

**Bottom Line:** Spinal ultrasound requires both paramedian longitudinal scanning to identify the level and transverse scanning to assess the midline and measure the depth to the epidural space, with key structures visible as specific echogenic patterns.

## 4.3. PRACTICAL APPROACH FOR ULTRASOUND-GUIDED NEURAXIAL BLOCK
### 4.3.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Clinical application of ultrasound for neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in ultrasound-assisted neuraxial blocks [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**A systematic approach to using ultrasound for identifying and marking the best insertion site for neuraxial procedures.**

1. **Perform systematic scan**: Start low (sacrum), identify L5-S1, count up using longitudinal view [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
2. **Optimize image**: Acquire best view of target interspace structures (ligamentum flavum/dura) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
3. **Center target**: Place desired interspace in the middle of the ultrasound screen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
4. **Mark skin**: Hold probe steady, mark skin at midpoint of both long and short axes of the probe [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
5. **Connect marks**: Remove probe, connect marks to form a cross indicating needle insertion site (midline or paramedian depending on scan plane used) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
6. **Measure depth**: Freeze image, use calipers to measure skin-to-epidural space (ligamentum flavum/dura) depth [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
7. **Estimate angle**: Visualize optimal needle trajectory based on image [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** A systematic approach to spinal ultrasonography involves identifying the target interspace, marking skin entry points, measuring depth to the epidural space, and estimating the optimal needle trajectory before performing the block.

# 5. NEURAXIAL ANESTHESIA TECHNIQUES

## 5.1. PATIENT PREPARATION AND EQUIPMENT
### 5.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Procedural aspects of neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and procedural competency [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Proper preparation and equipment setup are essential for safe and successful neuraxial procedures.**

### 5.1.2. EQUIPMENT REQUIREMENTS

* **Assemble ALL equipment** before starting: block supplies + emergency resuscitation equipment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Minimum monitoring**: Blood pressure (cycled frequently initially), pulse oximetry, ECG often standard [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Location**: OR, procedure room, labor room, ICU, holding area. Dedicated block area ideal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Dedicated cart recommended**:
	* **Neuraxial Supplies**: Sterile trays (spinal/epidural/CSE), needles (various types/sizes), catheters, LOR syringes, introducers, sterile prep, drapes, gloves, masks, caps, local anesthetics, adjuvants, labels [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Emergency Equipment**: Oxygen source, bag-valve mask, oral/nasal airways, laryngoscope (blades/handle), endotracheal tubes, supraglottic airway device, suction, IV access supplies, resuscitation drugs (**EPINEPHRINE**, **ATROPINE**, **PHENYLEPHRINE**, **EPHEDRINE**, **SUCCINYLCHOLINE**), **20% LIPID EMULSION** (for LAST) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Comprehensive preparation with appropriate monitoring, equipment, and emergency supplies is essential for safe neuraxial anesthesia; all necessary items should be assembled before beginning the procedure.

## 5.2. POSITIONING
### 5.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Technical aspects of patient positioning for neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Skills in optimizing patient position for procedure success [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Proper patient positioning maximizes intervertebral space opening and facilitates successful needle placement.**

* **Goal**: Maximize patient comfort, safety, and lumbar flexion to open interspinous spaces [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Common positions**: Sitting, Lateral Decubitus. (Prone jackknife rare) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Choice factors**: Proceduralist preference, patient characteristics (obesity, injury), procedure requirements (e.g., unilateral block) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 5.2.2. SITTING POSITION
* **Technique**:
	* Patient sits on edge of bed/table, feet supported on stool [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Relax shoulders ("slump"), curve back ("angry cat"), flex neck [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Assistant supports patient anteriorly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Tilting table forward (reverse Trendelenburg) may aid hip/lumbar flexion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Advantages**:
	* Midline easier to identify (esp. obese patients - C7 prominence to gluteal cleft line) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Easier for some proceduralists [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* CSF flow often more readily apparent for spinal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Disadvantages**:
	* Potential for vasovagal syncope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Less stable for frail/sedated patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* May be uncomfortable for patients with back pain [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Preferred by many for obese patients or those with poor landmarks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_5.png_4f3237.png_95a873.png_36fe8b.png)

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_6.png_41bcf5.png_bcdedb.png_b76454.png)

### 5.2.3. LATERAL DECUBITUS POSITION
* **Technique**:
	* Patient lies on side, back parallel to edge of bed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Hips and shoulders perpendicular to bed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Knees drawn up to chest, neck flexed ("fetal position") [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Pillow under head (and between knees if needed) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Assistant helps maintain position and flexion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Advantages**:
	* More stable for sedated/unstable patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Preferred for hip fractures, labor analgesia in active labor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Allows control over block height with hyper/hypobaric solutions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Disadvantages**:
	* Midline identification can be harder [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* CSF flow may be slower for spinal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Baricity considerations**:
	* **Hyperbaric**: Operative side DOWN initially [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Isobaric/Hypobaric**: Operative side UP initially [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Cesarean section**: Often right lateral initially, then supine with left uterine displacement after injection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Thin patients (BMI <25) often more comfortable laterally; obese (BMI >30) may prefer sitting [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_7.png_aef2d9.png_b6467e.png_40db7c.png)

### 5.2.4. PATIENT COMFORT
* **Use blankets/pillows** for warmth and support [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Light sedation**: Small doses of **MIDAZOLAM** or **FENTANYL** may be helpful [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Painful conditions** (e.g., fractures): May require small doses of **KETAMINE** or **PROPOFOL** for positioning [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* ⚠️ **Neuraxial anesthesia after induction of general anesthesia**: Generally discouraged in adults due to inability to detect paresthesias or pain on injection (increased neurologic risk). Considered acceptable in pediatrics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Patient positioning is critical for successful neuraxial anesthesia; sitting position facilitates midline identification and CSF flow but may be less stable, while lateral position offers greater stability and baricity control but can make midline identification more challenging.

## 5.3. SKIN PREPARATION
### 5.3.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Aseptic technique for neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Infection prevention competencies [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Strict aseptic technique is critical to prevent infectious complications of neuraxial anesthesia.**

* **Aseptic Technique is CRITICAL**:
	* Remove jewelry. Hand washing. Sterile gloves, caps, masks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
	* Use single-use antiseptic packets/applicators (**CHLORHEXIDINE** with alcohol preferred by many guidelines over povidone-iodine due to faster onset, longer duration, better efficacy in presence of blood/protein). Allow to dry completely [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
	* Apply antiseptic widely. Drape area with sterile drapes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* ⚠️ **Avoid contamination**: Previously opened multi-use bottles can be contaminated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Strict aseptic technique with appropriate skin antisepsis and sterile barriers is essential to prevent infectious complications of neuraxial anesthesia; chlorhexidine with alcohol is often preferred due to its superior efficacy and duration.

## 5.4. NEEDLES
### 5.4.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Equipment knowledge for neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical aspects of needle selection and use [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Understanding needle characteristics is essential for successful neuraxial procedures and minimizing complications.**

### 5.4.2. SPINAL NEEDLES
* **Tip design**:
	* **Cutting tip** (e.g., Quincke): Beveled tip cuts dural fibers. Higher PDPH risk. Bevel orientation parallel to longitudinal dural fibers recommended if used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Non-cutting / Pencil-point tip** (e.g., Whitacre, Sprotte, Pencan): Rounded tip spreads dural fibers. Lower PDPH risk. Preferred by most [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Gauge**: Smaller gauge (e.g., 25G, 27G) associated with lower PDPH risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Introducer needle**: Shorter, larger gauge (e.g., 20G or 22G) used to puncture skin/ligaments and guide fine spinal needle. Always insert introducer with bevel parallel to longitudinal axis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.3. EPIDURAL NEEDLES
* **Gauge**: Larger (e.g., 17G, 18G) to allow catheter passage (e.g., 19G, 20G catheter) and provide better tactile feedback [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Tip design**: Usually curved tip (e.g., Tuohy) to direct catheter away from dura. Straight tip (e.g., Crawford) sometimes used (e.g., caudal). Winged or non-winged hub. Markings for depth [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Features**:
	* Usually 8cm needle with 2cm hub, with 1cm markings to measure depth [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Huber tip provides blunt tip and guides catheter laterally [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Stylet prevents coring of tissue [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_8.png_f6fe39.png_8fb10e.png_dc647d.png)

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_9.png_ef817b.png_8e218c.png_4ccdb2.png)

### 5.4.4. COMBINED SPINAL-EPIDURAL NEEDLES
* **Needle-through-needle**: Long (e.g., 120-150mm), small-gauge (e.g., 25G, 27G) spinal needle passed through standard epidural needle. Spinal needle must protrude sufficiently (≥1.5 cm) beyond epidural tip. Specialized matched sets available [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Needle-beside-needle**: Separate skin punctures or specialized devices holding needles parallel. Less common [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_10.png_8a3d86.png_64974d.png_e90561.png)

**Bottom Line:** Needle selection significantly impacts success and complication rates; pencil-point spinal needles reduce PDPH risk, while Tuohy epidural needles facilitate catheter direction with their curved tip; specialized sets are available for combined spinal-epidural techniques.

## 5.5. APPROACH TECHNIQUES
### 5.5.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Technical approaches to neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Procedural skill in various neuraxial approaches [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Different approaches to needle insertion for neuraxial anesthesia address varying anatomical challenges.**

### 5.5.2. MIDLINE APPROACH
* **Most common** and straightforward for lumbar blocks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Needle insertion**: Midline of desired interspace, slight cephalad angulation (10-15°) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Structures pierced (in order)**: Skin → Subcutaneous fat → Supraspinous ligament → Interspinous ligament → Ligamentum flavum → Epidural space → Dura mater → Arachnoid mater → Subarachnoid space [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Obstruction**: Superficial bone = inferior spinous process; Deep bone = superior spinous process or lamina (if off midline) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 5.5.3. PARAMEDIAN APPROACH
* **Needle insertion**: ~1 cm lateral and ~1 cm inferior to the desired interspace (targeting inferior aspect of superior spinous process) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Needle trajectory**: Angled ~10-25° cephalad and ~10-25° medially, aiming for midline at depth of ligamentum flavum [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Structures pierced**: Skin → Subcutaneous fat → Paraspinous muscles (variable) → Ligamentum flavum (lateral aspect) → Epidural space → Dura/Arachnoid → Subarachnoid space. (Bypasses supra/interspinous ligaments) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Advantages**:
	* Useful if midline approach difficult (calcified ligaments, severe arthritis, prior surgery) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Often preferred for thoracic epidurals due to steep spinous process angle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* May avoid midline ligamentum flavum gaps [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Obstruction**: Bone usually represents lamina. Requires redirection (usually slightly more cephalad and medial) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Thoracic technique variation**: Intentionally contact lamina below target interspace to gauge depth, then "walk" needle cephalad off lamina into epidural space [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_11.png_aea533.png_667d1f.png_1d2879.png)

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_12.png_3c80c0.png_6782f9.png_390ac6.png)

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_13.png_5b6353.png_0710cd.png_f92fde.png)

**Bottom Line:** The midline approach is most common for lumbar blocks, traversing the interspinous ligament, while the paramedian approach is valuable for difficult anatomy or thoracic blocks, bypassing calcified interspinous ligaments and accommodating the steep angle of thoracic spinous processes.

## 5.6. SUBARACHNOID SPINAL ANESTHESIA TECHNIQUE
### 5.6.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Procedural technique for spinal anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in performing spinal anesthesia [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**A detailed approach to performing single-shot and continuous subarachnoid anesthesia.**

### 5.6.2. PROCEDURE STEPS
1. **Position** patient (sitting or lateral) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
2. **Prepare skin** aseptically and drape [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
3. **Infiltrate skin** with local anesthetic (e.g., 1% **LIDOCAINE**) using small needle. Optional deeper infiltration along needle track [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Avoid excessive local**: Can obscure landmarks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Optional*: Use finder needle to confirm interspace [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
4. **Insert introducer needle** (midline or paramedian) with bevel parallel to longitudinal dural fibers (up/down in lateral, side-to-side in sitting). Advance through skin and supraspinous/interspinous ligaments [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
5. **Pass spinal needle** (with stylet in place) through introducer. Advance slowly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
6. **Feel for tactile changes**: Increased resistance (ligamentum flavum), then distinct "pop" or give as dura/arachnoid is pierced. Pencil-point needles may have less distinct pop [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
7. **Remove stylet**: Check for free flow of clear CSF [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Sitting*: CSF should appear promptly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Lateral/Prone*: May be slower; gentle aspiration might be needed. Rotate needle slightly if no flow [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
8. **If bone contacted**: Withdraw spinal needle into introducer, redirect slightly cephalad (if midline) or adjust medial/lateral angle (if paramedian). Re-advance. Needle should advance deeper with each successful redirection off bone. If repeatedly hitting bone at same depth, reassess landmarks/level [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
9. **If blood**: If CSF tinged with blood but clears, usually safe to inject. If frank blood, reposition needle. Persistent blood may indicate vessel puncture; abort or try different interspace [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
10. **If paresthesia**:
	* *Transient paresthesia* common (needle touches nerve root). STOP advancing. Withdraw stylet [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* If CSF flows and paresthesia resolves, okay to inject slowly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Do NOT inject if paresthesia persists** or recurs on injection. Withdraw and reposition needle. Persistent pain requires abandoning that interspace [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
11. **Medication administration**:
	* Once free CSF flow confirmed, stabilize needle firmly with non-dominant hand against patient's back [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Attach syringe with medication securely (Luer lock) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Aspirate gently (~0.1-0.2 mL) to re-confirm CSF (swirling/birefringence seen with hyperbaric solutions) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Inject medication at appropriate rate (e.g., ~0.2 mL/sec). Avoid excessive force [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Optional*: Small final aspiration to confirm subarachnoid placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
12. **Remove needle/introducer** together smoothly. Apply sterile dressing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
13. **Position patient** appropriately for desired block spread based on baricity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_14.png_9630bf.png_977130.png_359515.png)

### 5.6.3. CONTINUOUS SUBARACHNOID SPINAL ANESTHESIA
* **Procedure**: After confirming subarachnoid space with appropriate (usually larger gauge, e.g., 18-21G) needle, insert spinal catheter 2-3 cm beyond needle tip [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Needle bevel orientation**: Directing bevel cephalad may facilitate catheter passage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* ⚠️ **Catheter direction**: Avoid excessive caudal insertion; associated with pooling and potential neurologic injury in past. Secure catheter carefully. Label clearly as "SPINAL" [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Successful spinal anesthesia requires careful technique with attention to positioning, systematic needle advancement, confirmation of CSF flow, appropriate management of paresthesias or blood, and controlled medication administration followed by proper patient positioning.

## 5.7. EPIDURAL ANESTHESIA TECHNIQUE
### 5.7.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Procedural technique for epidural anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in epidural anesthesia [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Techniques for identifying the epidural space, catheter placement, and confirming proper positioning.**

### 5.7.2. EPIDURAL SPACE IDENTIFICATION
* **Goal**: Advance needle through ligamentum flavum and stop precisely within the epidural space, avoiding dural puncture [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Common techniques**: Loss of Resistance (LOR), Hanging Drop [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 5.7.3. LOSS-OF-RESISTANCE (LOR) TECHNIQUE (MOST COMMON)
* **Principle**: Resistance to injection increases markedly within dense ligamentum flavum, then suddenly disappears ("loss of resistance") as needle tip enters the lower-resistance epidural space [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Procedure**:
	1. Advance epidural needle through skin, subcutaneous tissue, supra/interspinous ligaments until firmly engaged in ligamentum flavum (increased resistance felt) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	2. Remove stylet, attach LOR syringe (glass or low-resistance plastic) containing air or saline [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	3. Apply pressure to plunger while slowly advancing needle millimeter by millimeter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* **Intermittent pressure**: Advance needle slightly, stop, gently tap plunger. Repeat. Plunger will "bounce" back in ligament [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* **Continuous pressure**: Maintain constant pressure on plunger while advancing needle steadily. Requires careful control to stop immediately upon LOR. (e.g., Bromage grip, Doughty technique variations) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	4. Sudden ease of injection (plunger collapses) signifies entry into epidural space. Stop advancing immediately [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

	![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_15.png_c2f0d9.png_e591df.png_ed9dd7.png)

	![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_16.png_8b661f.png_8e6abc.png_fab0c0.png)

	![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_17.png_9720fb.png_574efc.png_0e9d9c.png)

	![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_18.png_17dd69.png_fbdc8e.png_812a63.png)

* **Air vs. Saline for LOR**:
	* **Air**:
		* *Advantages*: Clear visualization of CSF if dural puncture occurs. Less messy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Disadvantages*: Potential for venous air embolism (rare), pneumocephalus if dural puncture (causes headache), patchy block/unblocked segments (controversial), difficulty distinguishing LOR from false LOR (e.g., ligamentous cysts) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Saline**:
		* *Advantages*: May provide smoother LOR feel, potentially easier catheter threading (lubrication), may distend space slightly, less risk of pneumocephalus. Some studies suggest fewer unblocked segments or paresthesias (but meta-analysis inconclusive) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Disadvantages*: CSF return obscured if dural puncture occurs (dilution), potential for volume effect if large amount injected [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* 🔑 **HY**: Meta-analysis found no significant difference in major outcomes (PDPH, block failure) between air and saline LOR. Choice often based on provider preference and training [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Using small air bubble in saline syringe combines tactile feel of saline with visual confirmation of compression/LOR [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 5.7.4. HANGING DROP TECHNIQUE
* **Principle**: Relies on subatmospheric (negative) pressure within the epidural space (more reliable in thoracic region, less consistent in lumbar) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Procedure**:
	1. Advance epidural needle to ligamentum flavum [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	2. Remove stylet, place drop of saline in needle hub [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	3. Advance needle slowly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	4. As needle tip enters epidural space, negative pressure "sucks" the drop into the needle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Less commonly used** than LOR [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Negative pressure explanation**:
	* Likely due to combination of factors including communication between epidural and paravertebral spaces (which connect to pleural cavity in thoracic region) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* Pressure changes within the pleural cavity are transmitted to the paravertebral spaces and thence to the epidural space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* Pressure changes most pronounced in thoracic region but dissipated by epidural fat in cervical and sacral regions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* Also potentially produced by tenting of dura when blunt epidural needle presses against it during insertion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

### 5.7.5. EPIDURAL CATHETER INSERTION
* **After confirming epidural space**: Gently thread catheter through needle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Insertion depth**: Typically insert 3-5 cm beyond needle tip into epidural space [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Less than 3 cm*: Higher risk of dislodgement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *More than 5 cm*: Higher risk of catheter migrating laterally, anteriorly, or intravascularly/intrathecally. Increased risk of coiling or knotting [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Needle bevel orientation**: Influences initial catheter direction but doesn't guarantee final position (catheters often curl). Cephalad orientation usually intended [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Resistance during insertion**:
	* If mild resistance, try rotating needle slightly or injecting small amount of saline [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **If significant resistance**: DO NOT FORCE. DO NOT withdraw catheter back through needle (risk of shearing catheter tip). Remove needle and catheter together as a unit and restart procedure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Catheter removal from needle**: After desired depth reached, carefully withdraw needle over catheter, maintaining catheter position. Secure catheter with sterile dressing/tape [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 5.7.6. TEST DOSE
* **Purpose**: Detect unrecognized intravascular or intrathecal catheter placement BEFORE injecting full anesthetic dose. 🔑 CRITICAL SAFETY STEP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Common Test Dose Solution**: 3 mL of 1.5% **LIDOCAINE** with **EPINEPHRINE** 1:200,000 (contains 45 mg lidocaine and 15 mcg epinephrine) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Procedure**:
	1. Aspirate catheter gently: Check for blood or CSF. (Absence does not rule out misplacement) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	2. Inject test dose solution [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	3. Observe patient closely for 3-5 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Interpretation**:
	* **Intravascular injection**:
		* **Signs**: Sudden heart rate increase ≥10-20 bpm, SBP increase ≥15 mmHg, tinnitus, metallic taste, circumoral numbness, dizziness. (Epinephrine effect is key marker) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* ⚠️ **Limitations**: Response blunted by β-blockers, sedation, GA, advanced age, high existing block. Labor pain can cause false positives (tachycardia). Sensitivity/specificity are imperfect [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Intrathecal injection**:
		* **Signs**: Rapid onset (within 2-3 min) of sensory block (warmth/tingling in legs/feet, loss of cold/pinprick sensation) and/or motor block (difficulty moving feet/legs). Rapid pain relief in laboring patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* ⚠️ **Limitations**: Small intrathecal doses can still cause significant block. Some parturients report subjective warmth/weakness even with correct epidural placement (potential false positives) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Subdural injection**: Variable presentation, often patchy, delayed, unexpectedly high block for dose given. May mimic epidural or low spinal initially [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Negative Test Dose**: No signs of intravascular or intrathecal injection. Proceed with incremental dosing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: In pregnant women, careful aspiration and observation for rapid block onset are crucial, as epinephrine response can be unreliable [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* 🔑 **HY**: No test dose is 100% reliable. Catheters can migrate later. **Always inject subsequent doses incrementally** (e.g., 3-5 mL aliquots), aspirating before each increment and monitoring patient closely [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Epidural placement relies on loss of resistance to identify the epidural space, followed by careful catheter threading; the test dose is a critical safety step to identify intravascular or intrathecal placement before administering the full local anesthetic dose, with all subsequent doses given incrementally with repeated aspiration.

## 5.8. COMBINED SPINAL-EPIDURAL (CSE) TECHNIQUE
### 5.8.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Advanced neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in CSE technique [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium-High

---

**A technique combining the advantages of spinal and epidural anesthesia in a single procedure.**

### 5.8.2. NEEDLE-THROUGH-NEEDLE TECHNIQUE (MOST COMMON)
* **Procedure Steps**:
	1. **Position** patient, prep, drape, local infiltration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	2. **Identify epidural space** with epidural needle using preferred LOR technique (saline preferred by some to avoid air obscuring CSF; air preferred by others for clearer CSF identification) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* ⚠️ **Perform at L3-L4 or lower** to minimize conus medullaris risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
	3. **Pass long spinal needle** through epidural needle. Feel for resistance change as it exits epidural needle, then subtle "pop" as it pierces dura/arachnoid [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	4. **Remove spinal needle stylet**. Observe for free flow of CSF [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	5. **Stabilize both needles**. Attach syringe, aspirate CSF gently, inject subarachnoid medication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	6. **Carefully withdraw spinal needle** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	7. **Insert epidural catheter** through epidural needle as described previously (typically 3-5 cm) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	8. **Remove epidural needle** over catheter. Secure catheter. Test dose usually administered after initial spinal effect assessed or later [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_19.png_72b98d.png_111ccb.png_750231.png)

* **Common Problems**:
	* **Failure to obtain CSF**:
		* *Causes*: Spinal needle too short (esp. lateral position), epidural needle not truly in epidural space or angled laterally, dural tenting, obstruction by nerve root [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Troubleshooting*: Advance spinal needle slightly, rotate 360°, withdraw slightly, ensure epidural needle depth/angle correct, consider re-identifying epidural space. If unsuccessful, may proceed with epidural only or try different interspace [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Difficulty threading epidural catheter**: May occur if spinal needle created path deviation or if epidural space narrow [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_20.png_2971c9.png_a65ae8.png_4cae37.png)

### 5.8.3. MEDICATION EFFECTS IN CSE
* **Allows smaller intrathecal dose**: Spinal provides rapid onset, epidural allows supplementation/prolongation. May reduce hemodynamic instability from spinal dose [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Epidural volume effect**: Injecting saline/local anesthetic via epidural catheter can raise sensory level of existing spinal block ("epidural volume extension") [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Enhanced epidural spread**: Small dural hole may facilitate epidural drug movement into subarachnoid space, potentially improving subsequent epidural analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: CSE useful for procedures needing rapid onset but uncertain duration, or requiring potent postoperative analgesia (e.g., C-section, major lower limb ortho). Good option for morbidly obese where finding subarachnoid space can be difficult initially [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_21.png_c74692.png_44b160.png_8f4461.png)

**Bottom Line:** Combined spinal-epidural technique provides rapid onset from the spinal component with the ability to extend duration via the epidural catheter; the needle-through-needle approach is most common, with potential for enhanced drug effects due to the dural hole and epidural volume extension.

## 5.9. TECHNIQUE SELECTION CONSIDERATIONS
### 5.9.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Clinical decision-making for neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient-centered care and technique selection [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Factors to consider when selecting the appropriate neuraxial technique for a specific clinical scenario.**

* **Subarachnoid (Spinal)**:
	* *Pros*: Rapid onset, dense reliable block, small drug dose [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Cons*: Limited duration (single shot), higher PDPH risk (esp. with larger needles), abrupt hypotension risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Best for*: Surgeries of known, shorter duration (<2-3 hours) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Epidural**:
	* *Pros*: Titratable onset/level, indefinite duration via catheter, postoperative analgesia, lower PDPH risk than spinal attempt [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Cons*: Slower onset, larger drug dose (↑ systemic toxicity risk), potential for patchy/incomplete block, catheter-related issues (migration, infection) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Best for*: Longer surgeries, postoperative pain management (esp. thoracic/abdominal surgery - ERAS), labor analgesia, patients where slow onset hemodynamically preferable [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Combined Spinal-Epidural (CSE)**:
	* *Pros*: Combines rapid onset of spinal with flexibility/duration of epidural. Allows lower spinal dose. Good for post-op analgesia needs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Cons*: Technically more complex, combines risks of both techniques, potential for failed spinal component [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Best for*: Lower abdominal/extremity surgeries needing rapid start but potentially long duration or significant post-op pain. C-sections. Morbid obesity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Technique selection depends on procedure duration, need for postoperative analgesia, hemodynamic considerations, and technical factors; spinal is best for shorter procedures, epidural for longer cases or when postoperative analgesia is needed, and CSE when advantages of both are desired.

## 5.10. CAUDAL BLOCK TECHNIQUE
### 5.10.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Caudal anesthesia technique [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in caudal blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**A technique for accessing the epidural space via the sacral hiatus, particularly useful in pediatric patients.**

* **Patient and positioning**:
	* Confirm identity, site, indications and contraindications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Lateral position with knees drawn to chest (fetal position), or prone position [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Landmark identification**:
	* Locate the sacral hiatus by identifying the posterior superior iliac spines, which form the base of an equilateral triangle with the hiatus at the inferior point [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Sacral cornua can be appreciated as two adjacent knuckles approximately 5 cm above the tip of the coccyx at the upper end of the natal cleft [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Procedure**:
	* 22 gauge needle or cannula inserted slightly caudally at 45° through the sacrococcygeal membrane with a palpable pop [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Redirect the needle cranially but less than 2cm to avoid any dura [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* The distance from the tip of the dura to the membrane can be anything from 1.5 to 7.5 cm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Aspirate and inject local anesthetic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* There should be very little resistance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Correct needle position can be confirmed by lack of subcutaneous swelling, easy injection, and lack of resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
	* Needle can be placed incorrectly in several positions: subcutaneously, subperiosteally, within epidural vein, or within dura if advanced too far [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .
* **Dosing**:
	* 20-30 mL of local anesthetic in adults [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Pediatric dosing (0.25-0.5% bupivacaine or 0.125% in infants) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]:
		* 0.5 ml/kg for **sacral block** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* 1.0 ml/kg for **inguinal block** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* 1.5 ml/kg for **lower abdominal block** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Caudal block provides access to the epidural space via the sacral hiatus and is particularly valuable in pediatric practice; in adults, the distance from sacral hiatus to dural sac varies considerably, requiring careful needle advancement to avoid dural puncture.

# 6. PHYSIOLOGY OF NEURAXIAL ANESTHESIA

## 6.1. MECHANISM OF ACTION / SITE OF ACTION
### 6.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Basic pharmacology and neural blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Physiologic basis of neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**The primary mechanisms by which neuraxial anesthesia produces neural blockade.**

* **Primary site**: Nerve roots within the subarachnoid space (spinal) or diffusing across meninges from epidural space. Some effect on superficial spinal cord tracts also likely [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Spinal Anesthesia**: Local anesthetic directly bathes nerve roots and spinal cord surface in CSF [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Epidural Anesthesia**: Local anesthetic deposited in epidural space must diffuse across dura and arachnoid mater to reach nerve roots/cord. Some effect via leakage through intervertebral foramina onto spinal nerves [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Evidence**:
	* **SSEPs**: Reduced but not eliminated by spinal/epidural block, suggesting primary action on nerve roots with some cord conduction block [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **Epidural studies**: Show LA concentration gradient highest near injection site, decreasing further away. Drug found in CSF after epidural injection [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Neuraxial anesthesia works primarily by blocking nerve roots, with spinal anesthesia directly bathing roots in CSF while epidural anesthesia requires diffusion across meningeal barriers; some effect on superficial spinal cord tracts also occurs.

## 6.2. DIFFERENTIAL NERVE BLOCKADE
### 6.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Neurophysiology of nerve blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical manifestations of differential blockade [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**The sequential blockade of different nerve fiber types based on their varying sensitivity to local anesthetics.**

* **Concept**: Different nerve fiber types have varying sensitivity to local anesthetics based on size, myelination, and firing frequency [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Order of blockade** (most sensitive to least sensitive):
	1. **Autonomic fibers** (small, myelinated B fibers - sympathetic preganglionic): Blocked first and most extensively [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	2. **Sensory fibers** (small, myelinated A-delta for pain/temperature; slightly larger A-beta for touch/pressure): Blocked next [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	3. **Motor fibers** (large, myelinated A-alpha fibers): Blocked last and least extensively [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Clinical manifestation**:
	* **Sympathetic block** (assessed by temperature sensitivity) extends highest (often 2-6 segments above sensory) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Sensory block** (assessed by pinprick/cold) is intermediate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Motor block** (assessed by ability to move) extends lowest [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* *Example*: Patient may feel touch but not pain; may have leg weakness but still wiggle toes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Different fiber types have varying sensitivity to local anesthetics, with small autonomic fibers blocked first and most extensively, followed by sensory fibers, and large motor fibers blocked last and least extensively, resulting in sympathetic blockade extending higher than sensory blockade, which extends higher than motor blockade.

## 6.3. CARDIOVASCULAR EFFECTS
### 6.3.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Cardiovascular physiology of neuraxial blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical management of cardiovascular effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and hemodynamic management [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**The hemodynamic changes resulting from sympathetic blockade during neuraxial anesthesia.**

### 6.3.2. HEMODYNAMIC CHANGES
* **Primary mechanism**: Sympathetic blockade (T1-L2 outflow). Extent depends on block height [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Hemodynamic effects**:
	* **↓ Systemic Vascular Resistance (SVR)**: Vasodilation below block level (arterial and venous). Venodilation predominates → ↓ preload [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **↓ Cardiac Output (CO)**: Primarily due to decreased preload (venous pooling). Direct myocardial depression usually minimal with standard doses unless high block/preexisting dysfunction. *Initial studies suggested ↓CO, newer evidence sometimes shows maintained or slightly increased CO initially if preload maintained, followed by decrease* [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **↓ Blood Pressure (BP)**: Result of ↓ SVR and/or ↓ CO. Incidence/severity related to block height, speed of onset, patient factors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Heart Rate (HR) changes**:
		* **Variable**: May increase (baroreceptor reflex if BP drops), decrease, or stay same [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
		* **Bradycardia**: More likely with high thoracic blocks (T1-T4 cardioaccelerator fibers blocked → unopposed vagal tone). Also associated with ↓ venous return triggering Bezold-Jarisch reflex [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* ⚠️ **Severe bradycardia/asystole**: Rare but reported, especially with high block, hypovolemia, sedation, high vagal tone. Prompt treatment essential [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

	![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_22.png_bb30af.png_562a9e.png_c5cf0d.png)

### 6.3.3. RISK FACTORS FOR HYPOTENSION/BRADYCARDIA
* High sensory block level (above T6) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Rapid onset (spinal > epidural) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Advanced age [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Preexisting hypovolemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Pregnancy (aortocaval compression) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Obesity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Concurrent general anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Baseline low HR or high vagal tone [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Male gender (for bradycardia with epidural) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* β-blocker use [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 6.3.4. PREVENTION AND TREATMENT
* **Positioning**: Avoid aortocaval compression (left uterine displacement in pregnancy). Head-down tilt (Trendelenburg) for hypotension (transient effect, use cautiously) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **IV Fluids**:
	* *Preloading*: Largely ineffective at preventing hypotension unless patient hypovolemic. Modest volume (500-1000mL crystalloid) common practice but evidence weak [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Co-loading* (infusing fluid during/immediately after block): May be slightly more effective [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Avoid excessive fluids**: Risk of overload, especially in elderly or cardiac patients. Can damage endothelial glycocalyx (parturients) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Vasopressors**: First-line treatment for hypotension [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
	* **PHENYLEPHRINE**: Pure α1-agonist. ↑ SVR. May cause reflex bradycardia. Often preferred in obstetrics (maintains uterine blood flow). `Dose: 50-100 mcg IV bolus; Infusion 20-100 mcg/min` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **EPHEDRINE**: Mixed α/β agonist (indirect > direct). ↑ HR, ↑ CO, ↑ SVR. May cause tachycardia. Use cautiously if baseline tachycardia. `Dose: 5-10 mg IV bolus` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **NOREPINEPHRINE**: Potent α1, moderate β1 agonist. ↑ SVR, maintains/slightly increases CO & HR vs phenylephrine. Increasingly used in obstetrics. `Dose: 4-8 mcg IV bolus; Infusion 2-10 mcg/min` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **EPINEPHRINE**: For severe hypotension/bradycardia or cardiac arrest. `Dose: 10-100 mcg IV bolus for severe hypotension/bradycardia; standard ACLS doses for arrest` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **ATROPINE**: For symptomatic bradycardia. `Dose: 0.4-1.0 mg IV` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **GLYCOPYRROLATE**: Alternative anticholinergic if tachycardia less desired. `Dose: 0.2-0.4 mg IV` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Bezold-Jarisch Reflex**: Vagal reflex (bradycardia, hypotension, nausea) triggered by ↓ venous return [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **Treatment**: Fluids, vasopressors (ephedrine/epinephrine), atropine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Prevention**: Maintaining preload. Some evidence for **ONDANSETRON** (5HT3 antagonist) potentially reducing incidence [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Cardiovascular effects of neuraxial anesthesia result primarily from sympathetic blockade causing vasodilation and decreased preload; risk factors include high block level, rapid onset, advanced age, and hypovolemia; management involves appropriate positioning, fluids (co-loading preferred), and vasopressors, with phenylephrine and norepinephrine often preferred for their hemodynamic profile.

## 6.4. RESPIRATORY EFFECTS
### 6.4.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Respiratory physiology during neuraxial blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical implications of respiratory effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**The impact of neuraxial anesthesia on respiratory function varies with block height.**

* **Minimal effect** on resting ventilation with lumbar or low-thoracic blocks. Tidal volume, respiratory rate, resting PaCO2 usually unchanged. Phrenic nerve (C3-C5) controlling diaphragm is spared [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **High thoracic block**:
	* **May impair** intercostal and abdominal muscle function (muscles of expiration) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Subjective dyspnea**: Patients may feel unable to take deep breath even if function adequate. Reassurance is key [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Reduced cough effectiveness**: Can impair secretion clearance. Important in patients with respiratory disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Small decrease** in FVC/FEV1 possible, especially in elderly (>60 yrs) with blocks above T6 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* ⚠️ **High cervical block**: Can lead to phrenic nerve blockade → diaphragmatic paralysis → respiratory arrest. Requires immediate airway management and ventilation. Weakened hand grip can be an early sign [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Lumbar and low thoracic neuraxial blocks have minimal impact on respiratory function; high thoracic blocks may impair expiratory muscles, reducing cough effectiveness and causing subjective dyspnea despite adequate ventilation; high cervical blocks can paralyze the diaphragm and require immediate intervention.

## 6.5. GASTROINTESTINAL EFFECTS
### 6.5.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** GI physiology during neuraxial blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical implications of GI effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Sympathetic blockade leads to parasympathetic dominance affecting gut function.**

* **Sympathetic blockade (T5-L2)** → unopposed parasympathetic (vagal) activity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Effects**:
	* Increased secretions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* Relaxed sphincters [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* Increased peristalsis → small, contracted bowel (often beneficial for surgery) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Nausea and Vomiting (PONV): Common [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Causes*: Hypotension (gut ischemia), unopposed vagal stimulation, opioid adjuvants [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Risk factors*: Female gender, opioid use, high block, history of PONV/motion sickness. Strong link between hypotension and PONV during C-section [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Treatment*: Treat hypotension, antiemetics (**ONDANSETRON**, **DEXAMETHASONE**, etc.) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Hepatic blood flow**: Decreases proportionally with decreases in mean arterial pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Neuraxial anesthesia causes unopposed parasympathetic activity in the GI tract, leading to increased secretions, peristalsis, and contracted bowel (potentially advantageous for surgery); nausea and vomiting are common and often related to hypotension, requiring prompt treatment with vasopressors and antiemetics.

## 6.6. RENAL EFFECTS
### 6.6.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Renal physiology during neuraxial blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical implications of urinary dysfunction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Neuraxial anesthesia affects renal blood flow and bladder function.**

* **Renal blood flow**: Maintained by autoregulation if MAP remains within normal range. Significant hypotension can reduce RBF and GFR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Bladder function**: Lumbar/sacral blockade affects both sympathetic and parasympathetic control → urinary retention (detrusor hypotonia, inability to sense fullness) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Requires monitoring** for bladder distension, potential need for catheterization, especially if block prolonged or opioids used. Use shortest acting LA if no catheter planned [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Renal perfusion is preserved if blood pressure is maintained, but sympathetic and parasympathetic blockade of bladder innervation frequently causes urinary retention requiring bladder monitoring and often catheterization, especially with longer-duration blocks.

## 6.7. THERMOREGULATION
### 6.7.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Thermoregulation physiology during neuraxial blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical management of temperature effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Neuraxial anesthesia impairs thermoregulation through multiple mechanisms.**

* **Hypothermia**: Common due to several factors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]:
	* **Central inhibition**: Impaired thermoregulatory control in hypothalamus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **Peripheral vasodilation**: Below block level → heat redistribution from core to periphery → initial drop in core temp. Continued heat loss to environment [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **Blocked shivering**: Sympathetic/motor block prevents shivering below block level. Shivering may occur above block level but is often ineffective [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Consequences**: Patient discomfort, increased oxygen consumption (shivering), coagulopathy, delayed drug metabolism, increased risk of surgical site infection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Prevention/Management**: Forced air warming devices, warmed IV fluids, maintaining ambient OR temperature [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Neuraxial anesthesia frequently causes hypothermia through central inhibition, peripheral vasodilation, and blocked shivering, potentially leading to adverse outcomes; active warming measures are essential to prevent hypothermia and its consequences.

## 6.8. METABOLIC AND ENDOCRINE EFFECTS
### 6.8.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Stress response physiology during neuraxial blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical implications of metabolic effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Neuraxial anesthesia can attenuate the surgical stress response and related metabolic changes.**

* **Stress response reduction**: Neuraxial blockade can attenuate or block the surgical stress response (release of catecholamines, cortisol, glucose, etc.) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **More complete** for lower extremity/pelvic surgery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Partial suppression** for major abdominal/thoracic surgery (requires high thoracic block) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Potential benefits**: Reduced hyperglycemia, protein catabolism, possibly improved immune function (though clinical impact debated) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Neuraxial anesthesia can attenuate the surgical stress response, reducing catecholamine and cortisol release, which may reduce hyperglycemia and protein catabolism; this effect is more complete for lower extremity surgery than for major abdominal or thoracic procedures.

# 7. PHARMACOLOGY: SPREAD AND DURATION

## 7.1. SUBARACHNOID ANESTHESIA (SPINAL)
### 7.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Pharmacology of spinal anesthesia, drug distribution [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical aspects of spinal drug delivery and spread [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**The factors determining the spread and duration of local anesthetics in the subarachnoid space.**

### 7.1.2. KEY DETERMINANTS OF SPREAD
* **1. Local Anesthetic Characteristics**:
	* **Dose (Mass)**: Larger dose → greater spread, denser block, longer duration. Relationship not linear at common doses but becomes more apparent at higher doses (e.g., >15 mg **BUPIVACAINE**) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Baricity**: *Ratio of LA density to CSF density*. Most critical factor controllable by clinician [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Hyperbaric* (denser than CSF; mixed with dextrose): Spreads via gravity to dependent areas. Predictable spread based on position. Most commonly used [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Isobaric* (similar density to CSF; plain solution): Spreads via bulk displacement, local currents, diffusion. Less affected by gravity. Tends to produce longer block than hyperbaric. Spread can be less predictable [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Hypobaric* (less dense than CSF; mixed with sterile water/fentanyl): Spreads via gravity to non-dependent areas (floats up). Used less commonly (e.g., prone jackknife, specific unilateral blocks) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* Subarachnoid block with hyperbaric/hypobaric LA: Patient positioning is the most important factor in determining spread of subarachnoid block using heavy marcaine. Other factors have a smaller impact on block height: total dose, volume, concentration, injection site and barbotage.
		* Subarachnoid block with isobaric LA: Site of injection and total dose are most important factors in determining spread of block. Not affected by patient positioning
	* **Volume**: Less impact than dose or baricity, but larger volume may lead to slightly higher spread (bulk displacement) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Concentration**: Primarily affects density of block, less so the spread if dose (mass) kept constant [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

	![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_23.png_68d32f.png_d54812.png_3a6629.png)

	![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_24.png_3d2c61.png_0627d4.png_311066.png)

* **2. Patient Characteristics**:
	* **Age**: Older age associated with higher spread (decreased CSF volume/compliance?), slower onset, longer duration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Pregnancy**: Higher sensory blocks achieved with same dose (hormonal effects? decreased CSF volume due to epidural vein engorgement?). Sensitivity returns to normal postpartum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Height/Weight/BMI**: Generally considered to have **no clinically significant effect** on spread with standard doses, though extremes might play a role. Vertebral column length has minimal impact [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **CSF Volume/Density**: Lower CSF volume (e.g., due to dehydration, obesity, pregnancy) may lead to higher spread. Difficult to predict clinically [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Gender**: Female gender may increase motor block duration with isobaric **BUPIVACAINE** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Abdominal Girth**: Increased girth correlates with greater spread (likely due to decreased CSF volume from increased intra-abdominal pressure) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Spinal Anatomy**: Curvature (lordosis/kyphosis) influences spread of hyperbaric solutions. Scoliosis/prior surgery can make spread unpredictable [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **3. Technique Factors**:
	* **Injection Site**: Higher lumbar injection may lead to slightly higher block vs lower lumbar [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Patient Position (During and After Injection)**: CRITICAL for hyperbaric/hypobaric solutions. Less impact on isobaric [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Supine*: Hyperbaric pools in thoracic kyphosis (T4-T8) and sacral curve [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Sitting*: Hyperbaric pools in sacral region ("saddle block") [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* *Lateral*: Hyperbaric pools on dependent side; hypobaric on non-dependent side [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Injection Speed/Force**: Forceful injection can cause turbulent flow and higher spread (generally avoided) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Needle Bevel Direction**: May slightly influence directional spread (controversial) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Spinal curvature influence**: In supine non-pregnant adult, hyperbaric solutions settle at lowest curvature points: L4 in lumbar region and T8 in thoracic region; in pregnant patients these levels are L4-5 and T6 (ideal for cesarean section) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#4.3 Neuroanatomy (Central Nervous System and Peripheral Nervous System)|ⓘ]] .

### 7.1.3. DURATION OF SPINAL BLOCK
* **Primary factor**: Choice of local anesthetic agent [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Secondary factors**:
	* **Dose**: Larger dose → longer duration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Adjuvants**:
		* **Vasoconstrictors** (**EPINEPHRINE** 0.1-0.2 mg, **PHENYLEPHRINE** 2-5 mg):
			* *Mechanism*: ↓ spinal cord blood flow → ↓ LA absorption; possible direct α-receptor analgesic effect [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
			* *Effect*: Prolongs **TETRACAINE** significantly. Minimal effect on **LIDOCAINE**. Variable/modest effect on **BUPIVACAINE**, **ROPIVACAINE**. May increase risk of TNS with lidocaine.
