Less commonly used now due to limited efficacy with modern agents and potential side effects [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
		* **Opioids**:
			* *Mechanism*: Act on opioid receptors (μ, δ, κ) in spinal cord dorsal horn (substantia gelatinosa). Primary effect is to decrease nociceptive signal transmission by: (a) decreasing presynaptic Ca++ entry → inhibiting transmitter release, (b) increasing postsynaptic K+ efflux → hyperpolarizing cells, (c) inhibiting adenylate cyclase → reducing transmitter release. This blocks release of excitatory transmitters (glutamate, substance P) and enhances inhibitory pathways (glycine, GABA) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
			* **Key pharmacodynamic property**: Lipophilicity determines onset, duration, spread, and side effect profile. Most clinically relevant differentiator between agents [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
			* *Lipophilic* (**FENTANYL** 5-25 mcg, **SUFENTANIL** 1-5 mcg): 
				* **Pharmacokinetics**: Rapidly diffuse into neuronal tissues with high receptor affinity and rapid onset. Also diffuse into non-neuronal tissues (myelin, epidural fat) causing CSF concentrations to fall quickly, limiting duration and rostral spread [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
				* **Clinical effects**: Enhance intraoperative block quality, provide short-duration (2-4 hr) postoperative analgesia. Minimal rostral spread limits delayed respiratory depression risk [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
				* **Dosing**: Small doses (5-10 mcg **FENTANYL**) often as effective as larger doses with fewer side effects. No association with delayed respiratory depression in day case surgery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
				* **Side effects**: Pruritus, nausea (dose-dependent), early respiratory depression (if occurs) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
			* *Hydrophilic* (Preservative-free **MORPHINE** 50-200 mcg): 
				* **Pharmacokinetics**: Being hydrophilic, maintains concentration in CSF for longer periods, giving longer duration and greater analgesic spread above injection point. Slower onset compared to lipophilic agents [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
				* **Clinical effects**: Provide prolonged (12-24 hr) postoperative analgesia. Minimal effect on intraoperative block density due to slower onset. Significant rostral spread provides wider analgesic coverage but increases risk of delayed respiratory depression [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
				* **Contraindications**: Day case surgery due to delayed respiratory depression risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
				* **Side effects**: Pruritus (very common), nausea/vomiting, urinary retention, ⚠️ **Delayed respiratory depression** (peak 6-12 hrs post-injection, requires monitoring for ≥24 hours) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
			* **Spinal selectivity**: Ideally, intrathecal opioids would provide spinal analgesia without systemic effects. However, significant analgesic effect arises from systemic absorption, especially for lipophilic agents. Morphine shows highest spinal selectivity, fentanyl shows moderate selectivity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
		* **α2-Agonists** (**CLONIDINE** 15-150 mcg, **DEXMEDETOMIDINE** 3-10 mcg):
			* *Mechanism*: Act on α2-receptors in spinal cord → analgesia, potentiate LA effect [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
			* *Effect*: Prolong duration of sensory and motor block. Provide dose-dependent analgesia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
			* *Side effects*: Sedation, hypotension, bradycardia (dose-dependent). Dexmedetomidine more potent and longer-acting than clonidine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

💡 **Clinical Pearl**: For comprehensive information on intrathecal opioid pharmacology, clinical applications, and complications, see Section 7.5. INTRATHECAL OPIOIDS.

### 7.1.4. COMMON SPINAL ANESTHETIC AGENTS (PRESERVATIVE-FREE)
* **LIDOCAINE** 5% (Hyperbaric):
	* *Duration*: Short (~60-75 min) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: Historically common for short cases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **High risk of Transient Neurologic Symptoms (TNS)**. Use largely abandoned by many [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **BUPIVACAINE** 0.5% (Isobaric) or 0.75% in 8.25% Dextrose (Hyperbaric):
	* *Duration*: Long (~90-180+ min depending on dose/baricity) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: Most common spinal anesthetic. Reliable sensory block, motor block varies with dose [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Dose examples*: Saddle block 5-7.5 mg; Lower extremity/hip 10-15 mg; C-section 12-15 mg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **ROPIVACAINE** 0.5% or 0.75% (Isobaric or made Hyperbaric):
	* *Duration*: Similar to Bupivacaine, perhaps slightly shorter motor block [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: Alternative to Bupivacaine. Less cardiotoxic (less relevant at spinal doses). May have slightly less dense motor block [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **TETRACAINE** 1% (Isobaric or made Hyperbaric):
	* *Duration*: Very long (can be 3-6 hours, especially with epinephrine) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: Less common now. Dense block. Slower onset than bupivacaine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **2-CHLOROPROCAINE** 1% (Isobaric - specific formulation for intrathecal use):
	* *Duration*: Very short (~40-60 min). Rapid onset [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: For very short outpatient procedures where rapid recovery desired. Low TNS risk compared to lidocaine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Spinal anesthetic spread is primarily determined by baricity and patient position, with dose having a variable impact; duration depends on the choice of local anesthetic and can be extended with adjuvants, with bupivacaine being most commonly used due to its reliable block and moderate duration.

## 7.2. EPIDURAL ANESTHESIA
### 7.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Pharmacology of epidural anesthesia, drug distribution [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical aspects of epidural drug delivery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**The factors determining the spread, onset, and duration of local anesthetics in the epidural space.**

### 7.2.2. KEY DETERMINANTS OF SPREAD
* **1. Volume of Injectate**: Major determinant of spread. Larger volume → more segments blocked. General rule of thumb: 1-2 mL per segment to be blocked in adults (highly variable) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **2. Dose (Mass) of Drug**: Influences density and duration primarily, but also contributes to spread (higher mass in same volume = denser block, slightly less spread; higher mass via more volume = more spread) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **3. Injection Site**: CRITICAL factor. Block spreads both cephalad and caudad from injection site, but often more cephalad from lumbar injection. Thoracic injection provides more localized/segmental block [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Lumbar injection*: Spreads more cephalad than caudad [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Thoracic injection*: More symmetrical spread, or slightly more caudad [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **4. Patient Characteristics**:
	* **Age**: Older age → greater spread for given volume (decreased epidural space compliance? leaky intervertebral foramina?). Increased risk of hypotension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Pregnancy/Obesity**: Enhanced spread (decreased epidural space volume due to vein engorgement/fat). Requires dose reduction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Height/Weight/BMI**: Minimal clinically significant effect on segmental spread per mL injected [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Anatomy**: Spinal stenosis, prior surgery can impede or cause unpredictable spread [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **5. Technique Factors**:
	* **Injection Speed**: Rapid injection may slightly increase spread (controversial). Slow, incremental injection standard for safety [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Catheter Tip Position**: Influences distribution but precise location often uncertain. Multiorifice catheters may provide wider spread [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Patient Position**: Minimal effect on spread compared to spinal, but may slightly influence distribution if large volumes used slowly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 7.2.3. ONSET AND DURATION
* **Onset**: Slower than spinal (5-30 minutes for detectable sensory block, full effect takes longer). Depends on LA choice [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Duration**: Depends on LA choice and use of adjuvants. Can be maintained indefinitely with continuous infusion or intermittent boluses via catheter [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Regression**: Block recedes gradually. Typically measured by "two-segment regression" time [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Maintenance**:
	* *Intermittent bolus*: Inject ~1/2 to 2/3 initial dose when block regresses by 2 segments or at fixed intervals (e.g., every 60-120 min depending on LA) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Continuous infusion*: Provides stable block level. Common rates: 4-12 mL/hr (lumbar), lower for thoracic. Often uses dilute LA + opioid. Patient-Controlled Epidural Analgesia (PCEA) common [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 7.2.4. COMMON EPIDURAL ANESTHETIC AGENTS
* **2-CHLOROPROCAINE** 3%:
	* *Onset*: Very rapid (5-10 min) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Duration*: Short (~45-60 min two-segment regression) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: Situations needing rapid onset but short duration (e.g., urgent C-section conversion, outpatient procedures). May interfere with subsequent action of bupivacaine or opioids (metabolite antagonism - controversial) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **LIDOCAINE** 1.5-2%:
	* *Onset*: Rapid (10-15 min) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Duration*: Intermediate (~60-100 min two-segment regression) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: Common for surgical anesthesia. Produces reliable motor block (can be disadvantage for ambulation). Tachyphylaxis (decreasing effectiveness with repeated doses) can occur [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **MEPIVACAINE** 1.5-2%:
	* *Onset*: Similar to Lidocaine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Duration*: Slightly longer than Lidocaine (~60-100+ min) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: Alternative to Lidocaine. Less vasodilation. Accumulates in fetus (less ideal for labor) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **BUPIVACAINE** 0.25-0.5% (0.75% generally avoided for epidural due to cardiotoxicity risk):
	* *Onset*: Slower (15-30 min) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Duration*: Long (~120-240 min two-segment regression) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: Very common for surgical anesthesia and labor analgesia (lower concentrations 0.0625-0.125%). Provides more sensory than motor block (differential block) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Cardiotoxicity**: High concentrations or accidental IV injection can cause severe cardiac arrest resistant to resuscitation. Max dose limits important. **Use only 0.5% max for obstetrics** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **ROPIVACAINE** 0.2-1.0%:
	* *Onset*: Similar to Bupivacaine, maybe slightly faster [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Duration*: Long (~90-180 min two-segment regression), slightly shorter than Bupivacaine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Use*: Common alternative to Bupivacaine. Less cardiotoxic. Produces slightly less motor block than Bupivacaine at equivalent analgesic concentrations (useful for labor/ambulation) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **LEVOBUPICAINE** 0.25-0.5%:
	* *Onset/Duration/Use*: S-enantiomer of Bupivacaine. Similar profile but potentially less cardiotoxic. Less commonly available/used than Ropivacaine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 7.2.5. EPIDURAL ADJUVANTS
* **EPINEPHRINE** (e.g., 1:200,000 = 5 mcg/mL):
	* *Mechanism*: Vasoconstriction → ↓ systemic absorption of LA → prolongs duration (esp. for lidocaine/mepivacaine, less effect on bupivacaine/ropivacaine). May improve block density (α2-agonist effect). Marker for intravascular injection (test dose) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Effect*: Prolongs lidocaine/mepivacaine duration by ~40-80%. Improves quality/reduces failure rate (e.g., adding to lidocaine for C-section reduces pain/conversion to GA) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Opioids** (**FENTANYL** 1-5 mcg/mL, **SUFENTANIL** 0.2-1 mcg/mL, **HYDROMORPHONE** 10-20 mcg/mL, **MORPHINE** 2-5 mg total dose):
	* *Mechanism*: Act on spinal cord opioid receptors. Synergistic analgesia with LA. Allows lower LA concentration (reduces motor block/hypotension) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* *Effect*: Improve block quality, speed onset slightly, provide postoperative analgesia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Side effects*: Pruritus, nausea/vomiting, sedation, urinary retention. ⚠️ **MORPHINE**: Delayed respiratory depression risk (requires monitoring) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **α2-Agonists** (**CLONIDINE** 1-2 mcg/mL infusion, **DEXMEDETOMIDINE**):
	* *Mechanism*: Spinal α2-receptor activation → analgesia, potentiate LA [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* *Effect*: Prolong analgesia, intensify block [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Side effects*: Sedation, hypotension, bradycardia. Use less common than opioids [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Sodium Bicarbonate**:
	* *Mechanism*: Increases pH of LA solution → increases proportion of non-ionized (lipid-soluble) form → faster diffusion across nerve sheath [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* *Effect*: Speeds onset, increases density, especially for **LIDOCAINE**, **MEPIVACAINE**, **CHLOROPROCAINE** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Precipitation risk**: Especially with **BUPIVACAINE**. Use specific recipes carefully. `Dose: e.g., 1 mEq per 10 mL of lidocaine` [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Epidural spread is primarily determined by volume, injection site, and patient factors (age, pregnancy), with slower onset but longer duration than spinal anesthesia; commonly used agents include chloroprocaine (rapid onset, short duration), lidocaine (intermediate), and bupivacaine/ropivacaine (slow onset, long duration); adjuvants like opioids improve analgesia quality while allowing lower local anesthetic concentration.

## 7.3. INTRATHECAL OPIOIDS
### 7.3.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Pharmacology of intrathecal opioids [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical applications and complications of neuraxial opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**The use of opioids injected directly into the cerebrospinal fluid to provide enhanced analgesia during and after surgery.**

### 7.3.2. HISTORICAL BACKGROUND
* **1901**: Nicolae Racoviceanu-Piteşti first described intrathecal opioid use for analgesia in Paris [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **1970s**: Field expanded significantly after opioid receptors identified in spinal cord dorsal horn and proven to modulate nociceptive input [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **1979**: Wang described successful use of intrathecal morphine in eight patients with genitourinary malignancies, marking modern clinical application [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Evolution**: From experimental technique to standard adjuvant in neuraxial anesthesia and chronic pain management [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.3. MECHANISM OF ACTION
* **Primary effect**: Decrease or eliminate nociceptive signal transmission in spinal cord dorsal horn [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Receptor distribution**: Predominantly presynaptic opioid receptors (μ, δ, κ) with fewer postsynaptic receptors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Cellular mechanisms**:
	* **Presynaptic**: Decrease Ca++ entry → inhibit neurotransmitter release [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **Postsynaptic**: Increase K+ efflux → hyperpolarize neurons [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **Biochemical**: Inhibit adenylate cyclase → reduce transmitter release [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Net effect**: Decreased release of excitatory transmitters (glutamate, substance P) and enhanced inhibitory pathways (glycine, GABA) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Spinal vs systemic**: While midbrain descending pathways also contribute, the signal-blocking effect at spinal level is integral to intrathecal opioid analgesia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.4. PHARMACODYNAMICS AND CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS
* **Most clinically relevant property**: Degree of lipophilicity determines efficacy, onset, duration, spread, and side effect profile [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

**Lipophilic Agents (FENTANYL, SUFENTANIL)**:
* **Advantages**: Rapid onset, high receptor affinity, limited rostral spread (reduced delayed respiratory depression risk) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Disadvantages**: Rapid diffusion into non-neuronal tissues → quick CSF concentration decline → shorter duration and limited cephalad spread [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Clinical use**: Enhanced intraoperative block quality, short-duration postoperative analgesia (2-4 hours) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Hydrophilic Agents (MORPHINE)**:
* **Advantages**: Maintains CSF concentration longer → prolonged analgesia (12-24 hours) and greater analgesic spread above injection point [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Disadvantages**: Slower onset, prolonged action with potential for delayed respiratory depression [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Clinical use**: Prolonged postoperative analgesia, particularly valuable for major surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.5. SPINAL SELECTIVITY
* **Ideal goal**: Spinal analgesia without systemic side effects [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Reality**: Significant analgesic effect arises from systemic absorption, especially for lipophilic agents [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Selectivity ranking** (highest to lowest):
	* **Morphine**: Highest spinal selectivity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **Fentanyl**: Moderate spinal selectivity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* **Other agents**: Generally lower selectivity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.6. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS
**Perioperative Acute Pain Management**:

* **Day case surgery**:
	* **Agent of choice**: Fentanyl (no delayed respiratory depression risk) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Effects**: Synergistic with local anesthetics, improves block quality, minimal duration prolongation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Contraindication**: Intrathecal morphine due to delayed respiratory depression risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

* **Major joint replacement**:
	* **Hip replacement**: Morphine 100-200 mcg improves satisfaction, decreases PCA usage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Knee replacement**: Generally requires higher analgesic doses than hip. Morphine doses >300 mcg not recommended due to side effect risk exceeding benefit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Elderly patients**: Consider lower end of dosing range (100 mcg) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

* **Obstetric practice**:
	* **Cesarean section**: Morphine 100 mcg standard dose added to spinal anesthetic. Higher doses increase side effects without significant analgesic benefit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Adjuvant use**: Fentanyl may be added to decrease local anesthetic requirements and improve block quality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

* **Urological surgery**:
	* **TURP**: Morphine 50 mcg effective for reducing detrusor spasm postoperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Radical prostatectomy**: Multiple studies show decreased supplemental analgesic requirements in first 24 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Nephrectomy**: Morphine 300-500 mcg effective but requires monitoring for side effects at higher doses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

* **Major laparotomy**:
	* **Benefit**: Definite reduction in pain scores first 24 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Limitation**: Analgesic effect limited to first 24 hours; doesn't decrease hospital stay as monotherapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Dosing challenge**: Higher doses (300-400 mcg up to 7-10 mcg/kg) may be needed but optimal dose vs. side effect profile not definitively established [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

* **Thoracotomy**:
	* **Comparison**: Better analgesia with ITM + IV PCA vs. PCA alone, but thoracic epidural or paravertebral catheters provide superior analgesia with lower respiratory depression risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Role**: Second-line technique when epidural contraindicated or as adjuvant to paravertebral catheters [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

* **Spinal surgery**:
	* **Dosing**: 3-5 mcg/kg morphine alongside IV PCA effective for this painful procedure while minimizing respiratory depression risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Chronic Cancer Pain**:
* **Indication**: Refractory cancer pain or intolerable side effects from systemic opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Delivery methods**: Single injection → Short-term catheter → Long-term tunneled catheter → Implantable pump system [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Agent selection**: Morphine most common; hydromorphone if morphine ineffective/intolerated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Combination therapy**: May combine with low-dose local anesthetic, α2-agonists (clonidine), GABA agonists, or other agents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.7. COMPLICATIONS AND SIDE EFFECTS
**Respiratory Depression**:
* **Most feared complication**: Risk varies by agent and dose [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Timing**:
	* **Lipophilic agents**: Early onset (0.5-2 hours), short duration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Morphine**: Delayed onset (peak 6-12 hours), prolonged risk requiring ≥24-hour monitoring [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Mechanism**: Rostral spread to brainstem respiratory centers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Risk factors**: Age >65 years, morphine dose >300 mcg, concomitant systemic opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Monitoring**: ASA guidelines recommend hourly monitoring for 12 hours, then 2-hourly for following 12 hours after intrathecal morphine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Detection challenges**: May manifest as decreased respiratory rate with normal tidal volume OR normal rate with decreased tidal volume and rising CO2 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Management**: Naloxone. Patients requiring >300 mcg morphine should be monitored in high-dependency setting [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Pruritus**:
* **Incidence**: 5.1-85% (extremely variable) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Most common side effect**, particularly with morphine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Treatment options**:
	* **First-line**: Ondansetron (proven effective) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Naloxone infusion**: <2 mcg/kg/hr may be effective without affecting analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Antihistamines**: Limited effectiveness for this type of pruritus but sedating effects may interrupt itch-scratch cycle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Subhypnotic propofol**: Shown to be effective [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Nausea and Vomiting**:
* **Incidence**: Equivalent to systemic opioid administration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Agent variation**: Lipophilic opioids (fentanyl, sufentanil) appear non-causative [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Treatment**: Standard antiemetics; ondansetron particularly useful due to additional anti-pruritic effect [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Urinary Retention**:
* **Common with morphine**, uncommon with lipophilic opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Risk**: Detrusor muscle damage from undiagnosed distended bladder [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Management**: Monitor for retention, catheterize if occurs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Other Side Effects**:
* **Sedation, delayed gastric emptying, sweating**: No more problematic than systemic opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.8. DOSING RECOMMENDATIONS BY PROCEDURE
**Preservative-free preparations must be used for intrathecal injection** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

| **Procedure** | **Morphine Dose** | **Fentanyl Alternative** | **Special Considerations** |
|---------------|-------------------|---------------------------|----------------------------|
| **Day case surgery** | Contraindicated | 5-25 mcg | No delayed respiratory depression |
| **Hip replacement** | 100-200 mcg | 10-15 mcg | Lower dose for elderly (100 mcg) |
| **Knee replacement** | 150-200 mcg | 10-20 mcg | Higher analgesic requirements than hip |
| **Cesarean section** | 100 mcg | 10-15 mcg | Standard dose; higher doses ↑ side effects |
| **TURP** | 50 mcg | 5-10 mcg | Effective for detrusor spasm |
| **Radical prostatectomy** | 100-300 mcg | 15-25 mcg | Decreases 24-hour opioid requirements |
| **Nephrectomy** | 300-500 mcg** | Not standard | **Monitor for side effects at higher doses |
| **Major laparotomy** | 300-400 mcg** | Variable | **Optimal dose not established |
| **Spinal surgery** | 3-5 mcg/kg** | Variable | **Effective for painful procedure |

**Key**: **Doses marked with asterisk require high-dependency monitoring

### 7.3.9. SAFETY CONSIDERATIONS
**Absolute Requirements**:
* **Preservative-free opioids only** for intrathecal use [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **High-dependency monitoring** for patients receiving >300 mcg morphine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **24-hour respiratory monitoring** after intrathecal morphine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Contraindications**:
* **Day case surgery**: Intrathecal morphine absolutely contraindicated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Respiratory compromise**: Relative contraindication for morphine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Sleep apnea**: Increased vigilance required [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Prophylactic Measures**:
* **Naloxone infusion**: May reduce adverse events but optimal dosing to preserve analgesia unclear [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Routine supplemental oxygen**: Not recommended as may mask respiratory depression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Intrathecal opioids provide excellent perioperative analgesia through spinal cord opioid receptors, with lipophilic agents (fentanyl) offering rapid onset and short duration suitable for day surgery, while hydrophilic agents (morphine) provide prolonged analgesia but require intensive monitoring for delayed respiratory depression; dosing must be carefully tailored to procedure type and patient factors, with absolute contraindication for morphine in day case surgery.

### 7.3.10. ASA/ASRA GUIDELINES FOR RESPIRATORY DEPRESSION MONITORING
### 7.3.11. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Evidence-based monitoring protocols for neuraxial opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and standardized monitoring practices [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Updated ASA/ASRA practice guidelines (2016) provide evidence-based recommendations for preventing, detecting, and managing respiratory depression after neuraxial opioid administration.**

### 7.3.12. PATIENT RISK ASSESSMENT
**Pre-procedure Evaluation**:
* **Conduct focused history and physical examination** before administering neuraxial opioids `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Particular attention** should be directed toward:
	* Signs, symptoms, or history of sleep apnea `[ASRA 2016]`.
	* Co-existing diseases or conditions (diabetes, obesity) `[ASRA 2016]`.
	* Current medications (including preoperative opioids) `[ASRA 2016]`.
	* Previous adverse effects after opioid administration `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Physical examination** should include baseline vital signs, airway, heart, lung, and cognitive function assessment `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Increased Risk Factors**:
* Unstable medical condition `[ASRA 2016]`.
* Obesity `[ASRA 2016]`.
* Obstructive sleep apnea `[ASRA 2016]`.
* Concomitant administration of opioid analgesics or hypnotics by other routes `[ASRA 2016]`.
* Extremes of age `[ASRA 2016]`.

### 7.3.13. PREVENTION STRATEGIES
**Drug Selection Principles**:
* **Lowest efficacious dose**: Administer the lowest efficacious dose of neuraxial opioids to minimize respiratory depression risk `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Route considerations**: Single-injection neuraxial opioids may be safely used in place of parenteral opioids without altering respiratory depression risk `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Agent selection**: Continuous epidural infusion of fentanyl or sufentanil may be used in place of morphine or hydromorphone without increasing respiratory depression risk when clinically suitable `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Outpatient restrictions**: Do not administer neuraxial morphine or hydromorphone to outpatient surgical patients based on duration of action `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Drug Combination Precautions**:
* **Cautious administration**: Administer parenteral opioids or hypnotics cautiously in the presence of neuraxial opioids `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Enhanced monitoring**: Concomitant administration of neuraxial opioids with parenteral opioids, sedatives, hypnotics, or magnesium requires increased monitoring (intensity, duration, or additional methods) `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Sleep Apnea Management**:
* **Equipment availability**: Encourage patients with sleep apnea treated with noninvasive positive airway pressure to bring their own equipment to the hospital `[ASRA 2016]`.

### 7.3.14. MONITORING PROTOCOLS
**General Monitoring Requirements**:
* **All patients** receiving neuraxial opioids should be monitored for adequacy of ventilation (respiratory rate, depth), oxygenation (pulse oximetry when appropriate), and level of consciousness `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Enhanced monitoring** may be warranted for patients at increased risk `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Match monitoring duration** with drug pharmacokinetics `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Single-injection Neuraxial Lipophilic Opioids (Fentanyl, Sufentanil)**:
* **Minimum monitoring**: 2 hours after administration `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Continual monitoring**: First 20 minutes after administration (repeated regularly and frequently) `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Hourly monitoring**: At least once per hour until 2 hours have passed `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **After 2 hours**: Frequency dictated by patient's clinical condition and concurrent medications `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Continuous Infusion/PCEA with Neuraxial Lipophilic Opioids**:
* **Duration**: Monitor during entire time infusion is in use `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Initial phase**: Continual monitoring for first 20 minutes after initiation `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **First 12 hours**: At least once per hour `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **12-24 hours**: At least once every 2 hours `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **After 24 hours**: At least once every 4 hours `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Post-discontinuation**: Frequency dictated by clinical condition and concurrent medications `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Single-injection Neuraxial Hydrophilic Opioids (Morphine)**:
* **Minimum monitoring**: 24 hours after administration `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **First 12 hours**: At least once per hour `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **12-24 hours**: At least once every 2 hours `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **After 24 hours**: Frequency dictated by clinical condition and concurrent medications `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Continuous Infusion/PCEA with Neuraxial Hydrophilic Opioids**:
* **Duration**: Monitor during entire time infusion is in use `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **First 12 hours**: At least once per hour `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **12-24 hours**: At least once every 2 hours `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **After 24 hours**: At least once every 4 hours `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Post-discontinuation**: Frequency dictated by clinical condition and concurrent medications `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Extended-release Epidural Morphine**:
* **First 12 hours**: At least once per hour `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **12-24 hours**: At least once every 2 hours `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **24-48 hours**: At least once every 4 hours for minimum of 48 hours total `[ASRA 2016]`.

### 7.3.15. MANAGEMENT OF RESPIRATORY DEPRESSION
**Supplemental Oxygen**:
* **Availability**: Supplemental oxygen should be available for all patients receiving neuraxial opioids `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Administration**: Give to patients with altered level of consciousness, respiratory depression, or hypoxemia; continue until patient is alert and no respiratory depression or hypoxemia is present `[ASRA 2016]`.
* ⚠️ **Caution**: Routine use of supplemental oxygen may increase duration of apneic episodes and may hinder detection of atelectasis, transient apnea, and hypoventilation `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Reversal Agents**:
* **Availability**: Reversal agents should be available for all patients experiencing significant respiratory depression after neuraxial opioid administration `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **IV access**: Maintain intravenous access if recurring respiratory depression occurs `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Severe depression**: In presence of severe respiratory depression, initiate appropriate resuscitation `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Noninvasive Positive Pressure Ventilation**:
* **Consideration**: May be considered for improving ventilatory status `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Indication**: If frequent or severe airway obstruction or hypoxemia occurs during postoperative monitoring, initiate noninvasive positive pressure ventilation `[ASRA 2016]`.

### 7.3.16. EVIDENCE BASE AND GUIDELINE DEVELOPMENT
**Guidelines Development**:
* **Collaborative effort**: Developed jointly by ASA and American Society of Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Evidence categories**: Based on randomized controlled trials (Category A) and observational studies (Category B) `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Expert consensus**: Incorporates surveys of expert consultants and ASA members `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Key Evidence Findings**:
* **Route comparison**: Meta-analysis shows no significant difference in respiratory depression frequency between single-injection epidural opioids and intramuscular opioids, with less somnolence/sedation for epidural route `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Dose relationship**: Lower doses of single-injection epidural morphine or sufentanil reduce frequency of respiratory depression compared to higher doses `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Drug type comparison**: RCTs show no differences in respiratory depression frequency when comparing morphine with fentanyl or sufentanil by epidural or intrathecal route `[ASRA 2016]`.

### 7.3.17. SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS
**Sleep Apnea Patients**:
* **Enhanced monitoring**: Continuous pulse oximetry monitoring recommended after discharge from recovery room `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Monitoring settings**: Continuous monitoring may be provided in critical care/step-down unit, by telemetry on hospital ward, or by dedicated trained professional observer `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Duration**: Continuous monitoring should be maintained as long as patients remain at increased risk `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Patient Assessment During Monitoring**:
* **Sleeping patients**: In cases with other concerning signs, it is acceptable to awaken a sleeping patient to assess level of consciousness `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Multifaceted monitoring**: Assessment should include respiratory rate, depth of respiration (assessed without disturbing sleeping patient), oxygenation, and level of consciousness `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Bottom Line:** The 2016 ASA/ASRA guidelines provide evidence-based monitoring protocols that are agent-specific and risk-stratified, with lipophilic opioids requiring minimum 2-hour monitoring and hydrophilic opioids requiring minimum 24-hour monitoring; enhanced monitoring is indicated for high-risk patients, and protocols include specific recommendations for prevention, detection, and management of respiratory depression.

# 8. SURGICAL APPLICATIONS & DOSING EXAMPLES

## 8.1. SUBARACHNOID ANESTHESIA EXAMPLES
### 8.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Clinical applications of spinal anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in procedure-specific dosing [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Specific dosing recommendations for spinal anesthesia based on surgical procedure and required block level.**

*(Note: Doses are approximate and require adjustment based on patient factors, desired block height/duration, and adjuvants used. T = Thoracic dermatome level required)*

* **Cesarean Section**:
	* *Level*: ≥T4 (nipple line) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: **BUPIVACAINE** 0.75% hyperbaric, `Dose: 1.8-2.2 mL (13.5-16.5 mg)`. Or 0.5% isobaric `Dose: 2.5-3.0 mL (12.5-15 mg)` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Adjuvant*: **FENTANYL** `Dose: 10-15 mcg` +/- Preservative-Free **MORPHINE** `Dose: 100-150 mcg` (standard 100 mcg dose; higher doses increase side effects without significant analgesic benefit) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Reference*: See Section 7.5.6 for detailed obstetric intrathecal opioid considerations [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Postpartum Tubal Ligation**:
	* *Level*: ≥T8-T10 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: **BUPIVACAINE** 0.75% hyperbaric, `Dose: 1.5-1.8 mL (11-13.5 mg)` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Cervical Cerclage**:
	* *Level*: ≥T10-S4 (saddle) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: **BUPIVACAINE** 0.75% hyperbaric, `Dose: 0.8-1.2 mL (6-9 mg)`. Consider short-acting agent if outpatient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Hip Fracture/Replacement**:
	* *Level*: ≥T10-T12 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: **BUPIVACAINE** 0.5% isobaric, `Dose: 2.5-4.0 mL (12.5-20 mg)` (longer duration). Or 0.75% hyperbaric `Dose: 1.5-2.0 mL (11-15 mg)` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Position*: Often lateral, operative side up (isobaric) or down (hyperbaric) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Adjuvant*: Consider **MORPHINE** `Dose: 100-200 mcg` for post-op pain. Lower doses (100 mcg) for elderly patients. Proven to improve patient satisfaction and decrease morphine PCA usage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Total Knee Replacement**:
	* *Level*: ≥T10-T12 (higher if tourniquet used, T8-T10) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: **BUPIVACAINE** 0.75% hyperbaric, `Dose: 1.8-2.2 mL (13.5-16.5 mg)`. Or 0.5% isobaric `Dose: 2.0-3.0 mL (10-15 mg)` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Adjuvant*: **FENTANYL** `Dose: 10-15 mcg` (tourniquet pain). Consider **MORPHINE** `Dose: 150-200 mcg` (higher analgesic requirements than hip, but avoid >300 mcg due to side effect risk exceeding benefit) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Knee Arthroscopy (Outpatient)**:
	* *Level*: ≥L1-L2 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: Low-dose **BUPIVACAINE** 0.5% isobaric `Dose: 1.0-1.5 mL (5-7.5 mg)`. Or 0.75% hyperbaric `Dose: 0.8-1.0 mL (6-7.5 mg)`. Or **2-CHLOROPROCAINE** 1% `Dose: 3-4 mL (30-40 mg)` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Goal*: Adequate anesthesia with rapid recovery/discharge [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Ankle Surgery**:
	* *Level*: ≥L1-L2 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: **BUPIVACAINE** 0.75% hyperbaric, `Dose: 1.0-1.5 mL (7.5-11 mg)`. Adjust based on duration/tourniquet [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Notes*: S1 block crucial. Consider CSE or peripheral block for post-op pain [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Transurethral Resection (TURP/TURBT)**:
	* *Level*: ≥T10 (bladder distension pain) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: **BUPIVACAINE** 0.5% isobaric `Dose: 1.5-2.5 mL (7.5-12.5 mg)`. Or 0.75% hyperbaric `Dose: 1.2-1.8 mL (9-13.5 mg)` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Adjuvant*: **MORPHINE** `Dose: 50 mcg` effective for reducing detrusor muscle spasm postoperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Notes*: Patient awake to report symptoms of TURP syndrome (hyponatremia) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Inguinal Hernia Repair / Open Appendectomy**:
	* *Level*: ≥T6-T8 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: **BUPIVACAINE** 0.75% hyperbaric, `Dose: 1.8-2.2 mL (13.5-16.5 mg)` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Perianal/Perirectal Surgery**:
	* *Level*: Sacral ("Saddle Block") [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *LA*: **BUPIVACAINE** 0.75% hyperbaric, `Dose: 0.8-1.2 mL (6-9 mg)`. Keep patient sitting 3-5 min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Prone position*: Use isobaric or hypobaric **BUPIVACAINE** low dose [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Spinal anesthetic dosing varies by procedure type and required block height, with C-section requiring high thoracic block (T4) with larger doses (13.5-16.5 mg bupivacaine), knee surgery requiring mid-thoracic level (T10) with moderate doses, and ambulatory procedures often using smaller doses or shorter-acting agents for faster recovery. Intrathecal morphine dosing should be tailored to procedure type: 50 mcg for TURP, 100-200 mcg for joint replacement (lower doses for elderly), and contraindicated in day case surgery due to delayed respiratory depression risk.

## 8.2. EPIDURAL ANESTHESIA RECOMMENDATIONS
### 8.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Clinical applications of epidural anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Technical competency in procedure-specific dosing [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Guidance for epidural anesthetic administration based on surgical site and desired block spread.**

### 8.2.2. TARGET BLOCK SPREAD BY SURGERY TYPE
* **Thoracic Surgery (e.g., Thoracotomy)**:
	* *Insertion Site*: Mid-thoracic (T4-T8) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Target Spread*: T2-T8/T10 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Volume*: Smaller volumes needed (5-10 mL bolus). Infusion 3-8 mL/hr [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Upper Abdominal Surgery (e.g., Gastrectomy, Pancreatectomy)**:
	* *Insertion Site*: Low thoracic / High lumbar (T8-L1) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Target Spread*: T4-L1 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Volume*: Moderate volumes (10-15 mL bolus). Infusion 6-10 mL/hr [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Lower Abdominal / Pelvic Surgery (e.g., Colectomy, Hysterectomy)**:
	* *Insertion Site*: Mid-lumbar (L1-L3) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Target Spread*: T6/T8 - L4/S1 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Volume*: Larger volumes (15-20 mL bolus). Infusion 8-12 mL/hr [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Lower Extremity / Vascular Surgery**:
	* *Insertion Site*: Low lumbar (L2-L5) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Target Spread*: T10 - S5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Volume*: Larger volumes (15-20 mL bolus). Infusion 8-12 mL/hr [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Labor Analgesia**:
	* *Insertion Site*: Mid-lumbar (L2-L4) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Target Spread*: T10 - S5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Volume*: Initial bolus 10-15 mL dilute LA+opioid. Infusion 8-15 mL/hr (PCEA common) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Concentrations*: Dilute **BUPIVACAINE** or **ROPIVACAINE** (0.0625% - 0.125%) + **FENTANYL** (1-2 mcg/mL) or **SUFENTANIL** (0.2-0.5 mcg/mL) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 8.2.3. LOCAL ANESTHETICS FOR SURGICAL EPIDURAL BLOCK (APPROX. DURATIONS)
* **2-CHLOROPROCAINE** 3%:
	* *2-Segment Regression*: 45-60 min. *Resolution*: 100-160 min. *Epi prolongs*: 40-60% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **LIDOCAINE** 2% (+/- Epi):
	* *2-Segment Regression*: 60-100 min. *Resolution*: 160-200 min. *Epi prolongs*: 40-80% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **MEPIVACAINE** 2% (+/- Epi):
	* *2-Segment Regression*: 60-100 min. *Resolution*: 160-200 min. *Epi prolongs*: 40-80% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **ROPIVACAINE** 0.5-1.0%:
	* *2-Segment Regression*: 90-180 min. *Resolution*: 240-420 min. *Epi prolongs*: Minimal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **BUPIVACAINE** 0.5% (Avoid 0.75%):
	* *2-Segment Regression*: 120-240 min. *Resolution*: 300-460 min. *Epi prolongs*: Minimal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Epidural anesthesia requires careful selection of insertion site based on surgical location, with thoracic insertion for thoracic/upper abdominal procedures and lumbar insertion for lower abdominal and lower extremity surgery; volume requirements vary inversely with distance from surgical site, with smaller volumes needed for thoracic surgery and larger volumes for lumbar epidurals targeting upper dermatomes.

# 9. COMPLICATIONS OF NEURAXIAL ANESTHESIA

## 9.1. MINOR COMPLICATIONS
### 9.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Common complications of neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Management of complications [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Common, non-life-threatening complications that may occur after neuraxial anesthesia.**

* **Backache**:
	* **Common post-procedure**, usually localized, muscular, self-limiting (resolves in days) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Often attributed** to ligamentous strain from positioning, needle trauma, local anesthetic irritation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Postpartum back pain**: Common regardless of anesthesia type; epidural not proven to cause *long-term* backache, though association with accidental dural puncture exists [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Post-Dural Puncture Headache (PDPH)**: See detailed section below [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Nausea/Vomiting**: Discussed in Physiology section (hypotension, vagal) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Pruritus**: Common with opioid adjuvants (especially morphine, incidence 5.1-85%). Mechanism related to neuraxial opioid receptor activation, not histamine-mediated. Treatment options:
	* **First-line**: **ONDANSETRON** (proven effective and may also reduce nausea) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Naloxone infusion**: Low-dose (<2 mcg/kg/hr) may be effective without compromising analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Antihistamines** (diphenhydramine): Limited effectiveness for neuraxial opioid-induced pruritus but sedating effects may interrupt itch-scratch cycle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Subhypnotic propofol**: Shown to be effective in treating pruritus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Urinary Retention**: Common due to autonomic block and particularly associated with intrathecal morphine (dose-independent effect). Less common with lipophilic opioids (fentanyl, sufentanil). Monitor bladder volume, catheterize if needed. Risk of detrusor muscle damage from undiagnosed distended bladder. Resolves as block wears off [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Shivering**: Common due to thermoregulation changes. Treat with forced air warming, meperidine (small dose) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Transient Hearing Loss**: Rare, usually low-frequency, associated with larger spinal needles (CSF leak affecting perilymph pressure). Usually resolves spontaneously [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Minor complications of neuraxial anesthesia include backache (usually self-limiting), pruritus (especially with neuraxial opioids), urinary retention, shivering, and transient hearing loss; most resolve spontaneously or with simple interventions. Neuraxial opioid-induced pruritus is not histamine-mediated and responds best to ondansetron rather than antihistamines.

### 9.1.2. EVIDENCE-BASED RISK STRATIFICATION
**Patient-Specific Risk Factors** (per ASA/ASRA 2016 guidelines):
* **Sleep apnea**: Patients with obstructive sleep apnea require enhanced monitoring protocols and should bring their own CPAP/BiPAP equipment `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Co-existing medical conditions**: Diabetes, obesity, and other chronic diseases increase risk of complications `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Medication interactions**: Current opioid use, sedatives, or other CNS depressants require enhanced monitoring when combined with neuraxial opioids `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Age considerations**: Extremes of age (very young or elderly) may require modified monitoring protocols `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Procedure-Specific Factors**:
* **Choice of neuraxial agent**: Hydrophilic opioids (morphine) require significantly longer monitoring periods than lipophilic agents (fentanyl) `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Dose-dependent effects**: Higher doses of neuraxial opioids increase risk and duration of potential complications `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Route of administration**: Evidence shows single-injection neuraxial opioids may be safely used in place of parenteral opioids without altering complication risk `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Quality Improvement Considerations**:
* **Standardized protocols**: Implementation of evidence-based monitoring protocols can improve patient safety outcomes `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Staff education**: Proper training on recognition and management of neuraxial complications is essential [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Documentation**: Systematic documentation of risk factors, monitoring, and interventions supports quality improvement initiatives [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

## 9.2. INTRATHECAL OPIOID-SPECIFIC COMPLICATIONS
### 9.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Complications specific to neuraxial opioid use [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and opioid-related adverse event management [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Specific complications related to the addition of opioids to neuraxial anesthesia, requiring targeted management approaches.**

**Respiratory Depression**:
* **Most feared complication** of intrathecal opioids. Risk varies significantly by agent used and dose administered [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Mechanism**: Rostral spread of opioid in CSF to brainstem respiratory control centers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Timing and Agent-Specific Risks**:
	* **Lipophilic opioids** (fentanyl, sufentanil): Early onset (0.5-2 hours), short duration, limited rostral spread [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Hydrophilic opioids** (morphine): Delayed onset with peak risk 6-12 hours post-injection, prolonged risk requiring ≥24-hour monitoring [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Incidence**: Meta-analysis showed odds ratio of 7.86 with NNH of 15.6 for intrathecal morphine. Risk increases dramatically at doses >300 mcg (incidence 60-100% with 1-2.5 mg morphine) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Risk Factors**: Age >65 years, morphine dose >300 mcg, concomitant systemic opioid use [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Clinical Presentation**: 
	* May present as decreased respiratory rate with normal tidal volume OR normal rate with decreased tidal volume and rising CO2 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Pulse oximetry will detect desaturation but not hypercarbia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Monitoring Requirements**: 
	* **ASA Guidelines**: Hourly monitoring for 12 hours, then 2-hourly for following 12 hours after intrathecal morphine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **High-dependency setting**: Patients receiving >300 mcg morphine require enhanced monitoring environment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Avoid routine supplemental oxygen**: May mask respiratory depression and rising CO2 levels [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Management**: Naloxone administration. No patients in large meta-analysis required intubation, though some required naloxone for adequate ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Prevention strategies** (per ASA/ASRA 2016 guidelines):
	* Focused pre-procedure assessment including history of sleep apnea, co-existing diseases, current medications `[ASRA 2016]`.
	* Use lowest efficacious dose `[ASRA 2016]`.
	* Enhanced monitoring for high-risk patients (obesity, sleep apnea, extremes of age) `[ASRA 2016]`.
	* Cautious administration of parenteral opioids or hypnotics in presence of neuraxial opioids `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Pruritus**:
* **Incidence**: 5.1-85% (extremely variable) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Most common side effect**, particularly with morphine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Mechanism**: Related to neuraxial opioid receptor activation, not histamine-mediated `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Treatment options**:
	* **First-line**: Ondansetron (proven effective) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Naloxone infusion**: <2 mcg/kg/hr may be effective without affecting analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Antihistamines**: Limited effectiveness for this type of pruritus but sedating effects may interrupt itch-scratch cycle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Subhypnotic propofol**: Shown to be effective [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Nausea and Vomiting**:
* **Incidence**: Equivalent to systemic opioid administration with morphine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Agent-specific differences**: Lipophilic opioids (fentanyl, sufentanil) appear to be non-causative [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Treatment**: Standard antiemetics with ondansetron being particularly useful due to additional anti-pruritic benefits [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Urinary Retention**:
* **Common with morphine**, uncommon with lipophilic opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Risk**: Detrusor muscle damage from undiagnosed distended bladder [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Management**: Monitor for retention, catheterize if occurs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Sedation and Other Effects**:
* **Delayed gastric emptying**: No more problematic than systemic opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Sweating**: Similar to systemic opioid effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Sedation**: Dose-dependent, particularly with α2-agonist adjuvants [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Evidence-Based Monitoring Protocols (ASA/ASRA 2016)**:
* **Lipophilic opioids** (fentanyl): Minimum 2-hour monitoring with continual observation first 20 minutes, then hourly for 2 hours `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Hydrophilic opioids** (morphine): Minimum 24-hour monitoring with hourly assessment first 12 hours, then 2-hourly for next 12 hours `[ASRA 2016]`.
* **Enhanced monitoring settings**: Critical care/step-down unit, telemetry on hospital ward, or dedicated trained professional observer for high-risk patients `[ASRA 2016]`.

**Prophylactic Interventions**:
* **Naloxone infusion**: Some studies advocate routine low-dose naloxone infusion to reduce adverse events, but optimal dosing to preserve analgesia while reducing side effects requires further study [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Patient selection**: Avoid morphine in day case surgery, use caution in patients with sleep apnea or respiratory compromise [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Nausea and Vomiting**:
* **Incidence**: Equivalent to systemic opioid administration with morphine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Agent-specific differences**: Lipophilic opioids (fentanyl, sufentanil) appear to be non-causative [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Treatment**: Standard antiemetics with ondansetron being particularly useful due to additional anti-pruritic benefits [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Sedation and Other Effects**:
* **Delayed gastric emptying**: No more problematic than systemic opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Sweating**: Similar to systemic opioid effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Sedation**: Dose-dependent, particularly with α2-agonist adjuvants [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Prophylactic Interventions**:
* **Naloxone infusion**: Some studies advocate routine low-dose naloxone infusion to reduce adverse events, but optimal dosing to preserve analgesia while reducing side effects requires further study [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Patient selection**: Avoid morphine in day case surgery, use caution in patients with sleep apnea or respiratory compromise [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Intrathecal opioid complications are agent-specific and dose-dependent, with respiratory depression being the most serious concern requiring prolonged monitoring after morphine administration; while lipophilic opioids (fentanyl) cause early, short-duration effects, hydrophilic opioids (morphine) pose delayed respiratory depression risk peaking 6-12 hours post-injection and requiring ≥24-hour monitoring, with doses >300 mcg mandating high-dependency care.

## 9.3. POST-DURAL PUNCTURE HEADACHE (PDPH)
### 9.3.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Etiology and management of PDPH [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient care and complication management [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**A distinctive headache occurring after dural puncture, characterized by its postural nature and related to cerebrospinal fluid leakage.**

### 9.3.2. MECHANISM AND INCIDENCE
**Clinical Context**:
* The postpartum period is characterized by numerous changes such as sleep deprivation, irregular food intake and dehydration. Headache in the post-partum period is common, up to 39% in one prospective study with only 4.7% of headaches anesthesia related `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Epidural analgesia is commonly used to alleviate labor pain with a reported rate of over 50% at many institutions in the United States and over 85% in tertiary care labor and delivery centers with 24-hour obstetric anesthesia coverage `[ASA:PDPH]`.

* **Definition (International Headache Society)**: Headache occurring within 5 days of a lumbar puncture, caused by cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) leakage through the dural puncture. Usually accompanied by neck stiffness and/or subjective hearing symptoms. Usually remits spontaneously within 2 weeks, or after sealing of the leak with an autologous epidural blood patch `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* *Clinical Definition*: Headache occurring after dural puncture (intentional spinal or accidental epidural), typically postural (worse upright, relieved supine) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Mechanism**: CSF leakage through dural hole → ↓ CSF volume/pressure → caudal displacement of intracranial structures → traction on pain-sensitive structures (meninges, vessels, nerves). Compensatory vasodilation may contribute. Low intracranial CSF volume or intracranial hypotension may develop due to an intentional (spinal anesthesia) or unintended dural puncture (UDP) causing a loss of CSF into the epidural space through the dural hole leading to a fall in CSF pressure. The headache is postulated to be caused by traction on pain sensitive structures in the cranium; another etiology may be due to increased cerebral blood flow in response to decreased intracranial cerebral spinal fluid [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* **Subdural hematoma pathophysiology**: The proposed mechanism regarding the formation of an intracranial subdural hematoma after UDP is from decreased intracranial pressure placing traction on the bridging veins between the dura and arachnoid, resulting in their tearing and subsequent hematoma formation `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* **Incidence**: Varies greatly depending on needle size/type and patient population [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Epidural (Accidental Dural Puncture - ADP)**: 
		* Incidence of UDP: 0.51-1.5% in obstetric patients `[ASA:PDPH]`.
		* PDPH after UDP: 50-80% of patients may develop PDPH `[ASA:PDPH]`.
		* High risk (up to 50%+ with 17G/18G Tuohy needle) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Spinal**: 
		* PDPH incidence: 0.8-5% in highest pregnancy risk group `[ASA:PDPH]`.
		* Lower incidences for pencil point needle tip design and smaller gauges `[ASA:PDPH]`.
		* Lower risk with small gauge (25-27G), pencil-point needles (<1-2%). Higher with larger, cutting needles [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Timeline of CSF leak**: After dural puncture with 17-18G needle, CSF leak rate is approximately 0.3-1.5 mL/hour, with most holes sealing spontaneously within 24-48 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Compensatory mechanisms**: Initially, CSF production may increase and venous dilatation occurs to maintain intracranial pressure, but these mechanisms can be overwhelmed with significant leaks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Clinical Significance**: 
	* PDPH involved in 12% of obstetric closed claims `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* Postpartum morbidity following PDPH includes readmission (5.2%), increased relative risk for cerebral venous thrombosis (aOR = 11.4), and subdural hematoma (aOR = 76.7) `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* Increased incidence of persistent headache (aOR 6.4) and backache (aOR 4.4) one year later `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* Associated with increased incidence of postpartum depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, and decreased breastfeeding `[ASA:PDPH]`.

### 9.3.3. RISK FACTORS AND PREVENTION
* **Risk Factors**:
	* **Needle**: Larger gauge, cutting tip, perpendicular bevel orientation (vs parallel) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Patient**: Younger age (peak 20s-30s), female gender, pregnancy/postpartum, history of PDPH, low BMI, possibly history of chronic headaches [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Age, gender, previous history of headache, clinical experience of the provider also influence incidence `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **Procedure**: Multiple attempts, number of attempts, LOR with air (controversial) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* **Prevention**:
	* Use smallest effective gauge needle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Use pencil-point spinal needles (lower incidences for pencil point needle tip design and smaller gauges) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* Orient cutting needle bevel parallel to longitudinal dural fibers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Experienced operator. Ultrasound guidance for difficult anatomy. Factors influencing the incidence of PDPH include clinical experience of the provider [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **After ADP**:
		* *Intrathecal catheter placement*: Threading epidural catheter into subarachnoid space after ADP *may* reduce PDPH incidence or need for blood patch (controversial, data mixed). Evidence in support of leaving an intrathecal catheter in place for 24 hours after delivery, or infusion of intrathecal saline is equivocal. The risk of inadvertent epidural dosing of an intrathecal catheter and risk of infection should also be considered. Requires careful management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
		* *Prophylactic Epidural Blood Patch (EBP)*: Generally NOT recommended immediately after ADP; effectiveness uncertain (success rate not as high as therapeutic EBP), may obscure diagnosis later. PEBP is not routinely recommended at this time [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
		* *Other measures* (epidural morphine, saline, IV cosyntropin, caffeine): Limited evidence for prophylaxis. There is currently insufficient evidence to recommend prophylactic intrathecal morphine via an intrathecal catheter after UDP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.

### 9.3.4. CLINICAL FEATURES AND DIAGNOSIS
* **Onset**: Usually 12-72 hours post-puncture (can be immediate or delayed up to 5 days). Headache usually starts within 48 hours of an epidural UDP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* **Character**: Bilateral, frontal/occipital, throbbing or dull ache. **Hallmark: Postural nature** (worse sitting/standing, improved/resolved lying flat) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. ⚠️ **Important**: Postural component may not be present in up to 5% of cases of PDPH `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* **Associated symptoms**: Neck stiffness, nausea/vomiting, photophobia, phonophobia, tinnitus, dizziness, visual changes (diplopia - CN VI palsy). Subjective hearing symptoms may occur [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* **Recognition**: In one third of cases, an epidural dural puncture may not have been recognized at all `[ASA:PDPH]`. If a PDPH is suspected, a member of the anesthesia team should see the patient within 24 hours `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* **Diagnosis**: Primarily clinical based on history and postural nature.
