Imaging (MRI) usually not needed unless atypical features or suspicion of other pathology [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Diagnosis based on both clinical presentation (documented dural puncture and severe postural headache being most characteristic) and detailed history and physical examination `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **MRI findings** (if done): Diffuse meningeal enhancement, sagging brain, subdural fluid collections, pituitary enlargement (nonspecific) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Differential Diagnosis**: Caffeine withdrawal, tension/migraine headache, meningitis, sinus related, preeclampsia (parturients), pneumocephalus, intracranial pathology such as intracranial subdural hematoma and posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome (PRES) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* ⚠️ **Increased risk for intracranial subdural hematoma** after PDPH with incidence of 1.5 per 100,000 deliveries `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **PRES consideration**: Should be considered in patients presenting with headache, elevated blood pressure and proteinuria as this syndrome is seen in preeclamptic patients `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **Red flag features**: Patient presenting with severe unexpected, atypical PDPH features needs full neurological evaluation including neurology consult and radiological imaging (e.g., CT scan, MRI) `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* **Natural History**: Most resolve spontaneously within 5-7 days (if left untreated, resolves spontaneously in about 2 weeks in most women but may last longer in some women). Smaller gauge dural punctures with spinal anesthesia typically resolve in 2-3 days. Can persist longer. Increased risk of chronic headache after ADP. Emerging data from retrospective and prospective studies support an association between PDPH, especially after UDP with an epidural needle, with chronic headache sequelae [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.

### 9.3.5. TREATMENT
**Treatment Intensity Decision Making**:
* The intensity of maternal symptoms may dictate the need for an Epidural Blood Patch (EBP) `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* When PDPH is less severe, which may reflect a smaller dural tear with less CSF leak, conservative therapy may be preferred in the expectation the headache resolves without the need for an EBP `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* If headache is more significant such that activities of daily life and caring for the baby are compromised, an EBP should be considered `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* In those parturients with acute cranial nerve involvement (e.g., blurred vision, tinnitus, diplopia), an immediate EBP is recommended to prevent chronic complications `[ASA:PDPH]`.

* **Conservative (First-line)**:
	* **Conservative management**: Supportive therapy for the first 24-48 hours and consideration of a therapeutic epidural blood patch (EBP) if conservative management fails (PDPH symptoms not getting better or progressively worse) `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **Bed rest (supine)**: Symptomatic relief, doesn't shorten duration. Prolonged bed rest not recommended as it may increase risk of thromboembolic complications and is impractical in the setting of a parturient caring for a newborn [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **Hydration (oral/IV)**: Commonly recommended as part of rehydration but no proven benefit beyond supportive therapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **Analgesics**: Simple (acetaminophen, NSAIDs), opioids if severe. Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, aspirin, acetaminophen, and oral opioids (e.g., oxycodone) recommended [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **Abdominal binders**: May have no benefit or are impractical in the setting of a parturient caring for a newborn `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* **Caffeine (oral/IV)**: May provide temporary relief (vasoconstriction). Oral caffeine in the dose of 300-500 mg is recommended once or twice a day. Intravenous caffeine can be given if the parturient is unable to drink. Treatment with caffeine should not exceed 24 hours, oral therapy is preferred, and doses should not exceed 300 mg, with a maximum of 900 mg in 24 hours. A lower maximum dose of 200 mg in 24 hours should be considered for women who are breastfeeding, particularly those with low birth weight or premature infants. The effects of caffeine do not change the underlying course; it is only for short term symptomatic treatment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Pharmacological Treatment with Insufficient Evidence**:
* Other agents used with insufficient evidence include aminophylline, theophylline, adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), desmopressin (DDAVP), hydrocortisone, dexamethasone, methylprednisolone, triptans, gabapentinoids, methylergonovine, ondansetron, mannitol, and neostigmine and atropine in the treatment of obstetric PDPH `[ASA:PDPH]`.

* **Epidural Blood Patch (EBP)**: Definitive treatment for severe/persistent PDPH [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* *Mechanism*: Seals dural hole, restores CSF volume/pressure ("volume effect" + "patch effect"). Blood injected in the epidural space increases the subarachnoid pressure by causing compression of the thecal sac and forces CSF cephalad. The clot formed seals the dural puncture thereby preventing further leak of CSF [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* *Timing*: Often delayed 24-48 hrs after onset to allow spontaneous resolution, but can be done earlier if severe. Women should be informed that performing an EBP within 48 hours of dural puncture is associated with a reduction in its efficacy and a greater requirement for a repeat EBP. However, in severe obstetric PDPH, an EBP within 48 hours of dural puncture may be considered for symptom control, although it may need to be repeated. Blood patches placed after an initial observation period of greater than 24 hours have a higher success rate approaching 93% after one EBP and 97% after a second EBP. Success was greater with a longer interval between dural puncture and EBP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* *Success rate*: Complete and permanent relief of headache after one EBP in up to one third of women with PDPH following dural puncture with an epidural needle. Complete or partial relief may be seen in 50–80%. Up to 20% of women receive little or no relief from an EBP, even if repeated. Around 10% of those who received EBP had recurrence of headache within 24 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* *Technique*: Aseptic injection of 15-20 mL autologous blood into epidural space at or below level of dural puncture. Stop if significant pain. A volume of blood of 20 mL is recommended when performing an EBP. However, injection should stop before 20 mL if not tolerated by the patient. The major effect of an EBP appears to be within a few segments of the site of injection. Blood injected during an EBP spreads predominantly cranially. It is therefore recommended that an EBP is performed at the same level or one space lower than that at which the original dural puncture occurred [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* *Side effects*: Back pain, radicular pain (transient), mild temperature elevation. Back pain during and for several days after EBP is common and can be significant. Back pain during an EBP may occur in 50% of women, and at 24 hours post EBP, more than 80% of women may experience back pain. This can continue for several days but severity usually decreases over a few days, with resolution for most women by four weeks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.
	* ⚠️ **Rare complications**: Repeat ADP, infection, hematoma, arachnoiditis, seizures, nerve injury. Neurological symptoms may occasionally develop after an EBP, but their incidence is unknown and the relationship between an EBP and neurological symptoms may not be causative [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Second Therapeutic Epidural Blood Patch**:
* In cases of partial or no relief, a second EBP may be performed after consideration of other causes of headache. There is no evidence on the optimum time interval between the first and second EBP `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Where the diagnosis of obstetric PDPH is likely and an EBP has produced resolution of symptoms but headache subsequently returns, a second EBP may be offered as it is likely to be of benefit `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* If an EBP has produced some improvement in symptoms but the headache persists, a second EBP can be considered as it may be of benefit `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* In cases where an EBP has no effect on headache, or if the diagnosis of obstetric PDPH is less certain, or the nature of headache has changed, discussion with other specialties including obstetrics, neurology and neuroradiology should take place before a second EBP is performed `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* If two EBPs have failed to relieve symptoms, other causes of headache must be considered, and involvement of other specialties is recommended before performing a third EBP `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Alternative Invasive Procedures**:

**Prophylactic Epidural Blood Patch (PEBP)**:
* Up to 20 mL of blood is injected into the epidural space before catheter removal. The success rate of the PEBP is not as high as the TEBP, and may not prevent development of a PDPH, but may reduce the length and severity of symptoms. PEBP is not routinely recommended at this time `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Intrathecal Catheter Placement after UDP**:
* Following an UDP, an intrathecally placed catheter is left in situ for 24 hours. Evidence in support of leaving an intrathecal catheter in place for 24 hours after delivery, or infusion of intrathecal saline is equivocal. The risk of inadvertent epidural dosing of an intrathecal catheter and risk of infection should also be considered `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Other Alternative Invasive Techniques**:
* There is currently insufficient evidence to recommend the use of acupuncture, greater occipital nerve blocks, sphenopalatine ganglion blocks, epidural morphine, and prophylactic intrathecal morphine via an intrathecal catheter after UDP in the treatment of obstetric PDPH `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* An epidural patch using agents such as saline, epidural dextran infusion, epidural hydroxyethyl starch infusion, epidural gelatin, or fibrin glue has also been proposed but seldom used due to lack of sufficient studies. An epidural bolus administration of these agents may improve symptoms, but the effect is only transient `[ASA:PDPH]`.

### 9.3.6. CONSENT AND TECHNIQUE CONSIDERATIONS
**Consent for EBP**:
* Before performing an EBP, written information should be offered to women to aid the consent process. An EBP is a therapeutic intervention and written consent is recommended `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* There is a risk of further inadvertent dural puncture during an EBP and this should form part of the consent process `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Back pain both during and after an EBP is common, and in some cases severe, it should be discussed as part of the consent process `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Neurological symptoms may occasionally develop after an EBP. Given the severity of some neurological symptoms, their development should also be discussed as part of the consent process `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**EBP Technique Details**:
* A full aseptic technique should be employed for both the epidural component and venesection. The epidural space should be located before venesection is performed and a peripheral intravenous catheter placement may be preferred after venesection. Blood should be injected immediately into the epidural space through the epidural needle `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Throughout the EBP procedure, regular observations of maternal pulse, blood pressure and temperature should be recorded in the medical record `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Studies in vitro have shown both lidocaine and CSF have a detrimental effect on coagulation. Increasing concentrations of lidocaine cause hypocoagulability and fibrinolysis whilst CSF has both procoagulant and clot destabilizing effects `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Contraindications**:
* Maternal systemic infection and 'red-flag' symptoms suggesting an alternative diagnosis should be excluded `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* An appropriate amount of time should elapse before neuraxial instrumentation in women receiving anticoagulation therapy `[ASA:PDPH]`.

### 9.3.7. POST-EBP MANAGEMENT
**Immediate Post-EBP Care**:
* Immediately after an EBP, the patient should be kept in the supine position for up to 1-2 hours for best results `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Regular observations of maternal pulse, blood pressure and temperature need to be documented following the procedure. The frequency and duration of these observations should be decided by individual unit protocols considering maternal safety and health `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Patients should be seen before hospital discharge after the EBP. Women who remain in hospital should be reviewed daily until discharge or until symptoms resolve `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Failed EBP Management**:
* After a failed EBP, alternative causes of headache should be considered `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* If the headache changes in nature, neurological signs develop, consciousness level reduces, headache is atypical in nature or when two EBPs have been unsuccessful, urgent consideration should be given to further investigation and imaging and consultation with neurology or neurosurgery `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Patients with persistent symptoms after a second blood patch should be considered for a detailed neurological work-up before any further intervention `[ASA:PDPH]`.

### 9.3.8. FOLLOW-UP AND LONG-TERM CONSIDERATIONS
**Inpatient Follow-up**:
* All women who experience dural puncture with an epidural needle or PDPH after a spinal block should be reviewed daily by a member of the anesthetic team while in the hospital until symptoms have resolved `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Ideally, when a woman experiences PDPH, follow-up should continue until the headache resolves `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Post-Discharge Follow-up**:
* Following discharge from the hospital, all women who experience a recognized dural puncture with an epidural needle or have a PDPH diagnosed require follow-up, regardless of whether an EBP is performed `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* Follow-up should occur on a regular basis until symptoms resolve. Before discharge, women should be given verbal and written advice on when and how to contact the hospital should headache return, or if other symptoms develop `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Long-term Sequelae**:
* After 6 weeks, chronic headache (35%), backache (58%), and neckache (14%) sequelae persist in the obstetrical population after UDP `[ASA:PDPH]`.
* When parturients are considering labor epidural analgesia, long-term sequelae should be discussed in the informed consent decision-making process `[ASA:PDPH]`.

### 9.3.9. ASA SUMMARY RECOMMENDATIONS
1. All patients with a confirmed or suspected UDP or PDPH need a brief physical exam for the presence or absence of neurologic symptoms (e.g., cranial nerve function, motor reflexes). Patient follow-up should occur within 24-48 hours after an UDP `[ASA:PDPH]`.

2. In the first 24-48 hours, conservative measures for symptomatic relief (e.g., non-opioid analgesics, opioids for severe breakthrough pain, lying flat, adequate hydration) are recommended initially before initiating a TEBP. If the PDPH is severe enough or affects the mother-child interaction, a TEBP should not be delayed `[ASA:PDPH]`.

3. Conservative treatment may be used for a mild PDPH. TEBP can be used for definitive treatment `[ASA:PDPH]`.

4. For persistent or severe PDPH, the most successful intervention is a TEBP `[ASA:PDPH]`.

5. Some patients may require a second TEBP with a classic clinical history for PDPH and a partial or temporary response to the first TEBP `[ASA:PDPH]`.

6. In those parturients whose headache is not getting better or progressively getting worse, further neurological workup or neurological consultation including brain imaging should be considered `[ASA:PDPH]`.

7. Prior to a third TEBP, further investigation including a neurological consult needs to be considered `[ASA:PDPH]`.

8. All patients with confirmed or suspected PDPH should receive PDPH education regarding if symptoms occur or worsen, to follow-up with a physician `[ASA:PDPH]`.

**Bottom Line:** PDPH is characterized by postural headache occurring after dural puncture, with higher risk following large-needle dural puncture (e.g., accidental during epidural) but reduced risk with small, pencil-point spinal needles; conservative management includes bed rest, hydration, analgesics, and caffeine, while epidural blood patch is the definitive treatment for severe or persistent cases, with high success rates. ASA guidelines emphasize the importance of proper diagnosis, timely intervention, comprehensive consent, and systematic follow-up to minimize long-term sequelae and ensure optimal patient outcomes.

## 9.4. SERIOUS NEUROLOGIC COMPLICATIONS
### 9.4.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Major complications of neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Critical complication recognition and management [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Rare but serious neurologic complications that can occur after neuraxial anesthesia, requiring prompt diagnosis and management.**

* **Overall Incidence**: Permanent injury estimated ~1:20,000 to 1:200,000 blocks (varies by source/definition). Higher risk with epidural vs spinal, thoracic vs lumbar, presence of coagulopathy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Quality Assurance and Risk Mitigation**:
* **Pre-procedure assessment**: Systematic evaluation of contraindications, particularly coagulopathy and infection risk factors [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Standardized monitoring protocols**: Implementation of evidence-based postoperative neurologic assessment protocols [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Prompt recognition and intervention**: Early identification and management of neurologic complications can significantly impact outcomes [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Multidisciplinary approach**: Coordination between anesthesiology, neurology, and neurosurgery for optimal patient care [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

### 9.4.2. DIRECT NEEDLE TRAUMA
* **Spinal Cord Injury**:
	* **Risk**: Needle insertion above L1/L2 in adults (conus medullaris). Misidentification of interspace common. Thoracic/cervical blocks carry higher risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
	* **Presentation**: Immediate sharp pain radiating down leg(s), followed by sensory/motor deficits, bowel/bladder dysfunction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Prevention**: Accurate landmark identification (US helpful), insert below L1/L2, stop/redirect if paresthesia encountered [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Nerve Root Injury / Cauda Equina Syndrome**:
	* **Risk**: Needle contact with nerve roots (more common with lateral deviation or in cauda equina). Paresthesia during injection is warning sign [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Presentation**: Persistent paresthesia, radicular pain, sensory/motor deficits in specific distribution, bowel/bladder dysfunction (cauda equina syndrome) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* **Prevention**: Avoid injection if persistent paresthesia occurs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_25.png_36ad8f.png_f558bb.png_617f76.png)

### 9.4.3. SPINAL HEMATOMA (EPIDURAL OR SUBDURAL)
* **Incidence**: Rare in general population (~1:150,000 epidural, ~1:220,000 spinal). Risk significantly increased by anticoagulation/coagulopathy. Higher risk with epidural catheter placement/removal vs single shot spinal. Orthopedic population higher risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Mechanism**: Bleeding into epidural/subdural space → compression of spinal cord/nerve roots [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Risk Factors**: Anticoagulation (therapeutic > prophylactic), antiplatelet drugs (potent agents > aspirin/NSAIDs), coagulopathy (liver disease, DIC), thrombocytopenia, difficult/traumatic needle placement, indwelling catheter (especially removal), spinal stenosis, older age, female gender [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Presentation**:
	* **Hallmark**: New or progressive neurologic deficit after neuraxial block (sensory/motor loss, bowel/bladder dysfunction) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
	* Often preceded by sharp, severe back/radicular pain unresponsive to analgesics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Onset can be hours to days after procedure/catheter removal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Diagnosis**: **EMERGENT MRI** is imaging modality of choice [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Management**: **NEUROSURGICAL EMERGENCY**. Requires prompt diagnosis and surgical decompression (laminectomy) ideally within 8-12 hours of symptom onset for best chance of neurologic recovery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Prevention**: Strict adherence to anticoagulation guidelines (See ANTICOAGULATION section). Atraumatic technique. Vigilant post-procedure monitoring [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

### 9.4.4. EPIDURAL ABSCESS
* **Incidence**: Rare (~1:100,000 to 1:500,000). Risk increased with indwelling catheters, immunocompromise, systemic infection, diabetes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Mechanism**: Bacterial contamination during insertion or hematogenous seeding → infection in epidural space → abscess formation → cord compression. *Staphylococcus aureus* most common organism [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Presentation**: Triad of **fever, severe back pain, and progressive neurologic deficits**. Onset typically days to weeks after procedure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Diagnosis**: Emergent MRI with contrast. Blood cultures, inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Management**: Urgent surgical decompression + prolonged IV antibiotics. Neurologic outcome depends on severity/duration of deficit before treatment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Prevention**: Strict aseptic technique during insertion and catheter care. Remove catheter promptly if signs of local/systemic infection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_26.png_feb8c6.png_853e72.png_bd7bed.png)

### 9.4.5. ANTERIOR SPINAL ARTERY SYNDROME
* **Mechanism**: Spinal cord ischemia due to occlusion or hypoperfusion of ASA or critical radicular artery (Adamkiewicz). Can be caused by direct vascular trauma (rare), prolonged severe hypotension, vasospasm, thromboembolism, aortic surgery/clamping [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Presentation**: Acute onset of bilateral flaccid paralysis and loss of pain/temperature sensation below level of injury, with *preserved proprioception and light touch* (posterior columns spared). Bowel/bladder dysfunction common [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Prognosis**: Often poor neurologic recovery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![image.png](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_image_27.png_73e42e.png_d35b54.png_5a2f9d.png)

### 9.4.6. CHEMICAL INJURY / ARACHNOIDITIS
* **Mechanism**: Neurotoxicity from injected substance (wrong drug, wrong concentration, preservatives, contaminants like cleaning solutions). Can cause inflammation of meninges/nerve roots [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Historical issues**: Preservatives (sodium bisulfite in 2-chloroprocaine), detergents. Modern preparations generally safe if used correctly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **High concentrations** of local anesthetic (e.g., from continuous spinal microcatheters - now withdrawn) associated with cauda equina syndrome [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Presentation**: Variable; burning pain, progressive neurologic deficits, bowel/bladder dysfunction. Can be delayed onset [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Diagnosis**: History, MRI may show nerve root clumping/enhancement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Prevention**: Use only preservative-free solutions intended for neuraxial use. Verify drug/concentration carefully. Avoid contamination [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

### 9.4.7. TRANSIENT NEUROLOGIC SYMPTOMS (TNS)
* *Definition*: Syndrome of back pain radiating to buttocks/posterior thighs, occurring within hours to 24 hrs after resolution of spinal anesthesia, resolving spontaneously within days (usually <1 week). No objective neurologic deficits [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Strongest association**: Intrathecal **LIDOCAINE** (incidence up to 30%+). Also reported (less frequently) with other LAs (mepivacaine, bupivacaine - rare) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Risk factors**: Lidocaine use, lithotomy position, knee arthroscopy, outpatient status [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Mechanism**: Unclear; possibly direct nerve root irritation/inflammation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Management**: Reassurance, NSAIDs. Rule out more serious pathology if symptoms severe or persistent [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
* 🔑 **HY**: Due to TNS risk, many clinicians avoid intrathecal lidocaine, opting for short-acting alternatives like 2-chloroprocaine or low-dose bupivacaine/ropivacaine for short procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 9.4.8. CAUDA EQUINA SYNDROME
* **Mechanism**: Compression or injury to multiple nerve roots of the cauda equina [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Symptoms**:
	* Saddle anesthesia (perineal/perianal numbness) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Bowel/bladder dysfunction (incontinence or retention) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Sexual dysfunction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Lower extremity pain, weakness, and sensory loss (often bilateral) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Causes in Neuraxial Setting**:
	* Hematoma or abscess causing compression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Direct needle trauma to multiple nerve roots [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Chemical neurotoxicity (high concentration local anesthetics) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Management**: Urgent MRI to identify cause, neurosurgical intervention if compressive lesion, supportive care, rehabilitation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

![nse-3819496812822154713-111304.jpg](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_nse-3819496812822154713-111304.jpg_312cb9.jpg_659a36.jpg_44ece2.jpg)

### 9.4.9. TRAUMATIC CORD INJURY AND SYNDROMES
* **Brown-Sequard Syndrome** (hemisection of spinal cord):
	* Loss of fine touch, proprioception, vibration sense ipsilaterally below lesion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Loss of pain/temperature contralaterally below lesion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Ipsilateral motor weakness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Anterior Cord Syndrome**:
	* Bilateral loss of motor function, pain, and temperature sensation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Preserved vibration and proprioception [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Central Cord Syndrome**:
	* Bilateral sensory and motor deficit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Upper limbs affected more than lower limbs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Serious neurologic complications of neuraxial anesthesia are rare but potentially devastating; key concerns include spinal hematoma (neurosurgical emergency requiring prompt MRI and decompression), epidural abscess (presenting with fever, back pain, and neurologic deficits), direct needle trauma to cord or nerve roots, and chemical injuries; transient neurologic symptoms are more common, particularly with lidocaine spinal anesthesia.

## 9.5. SYSTEMIC TOXICITY OF LOCAL ANESTHETICS (LAST)
### 9.5.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Local anesthetic systemic toxicity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Emergency management of LAST [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**A potentially life-threatening complication resulting from excessive systemic levels of local anesthetics affecting the central nervous and cardiovascular systems.**

* **Mechanism**: Accidental intravascular injection or excessive systemic absorption (usually after high-volume peripheral nerve block or epidural). High plasma LA levels affect CNS and cardiovascular system [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Incidence**: Rare with spinal (tiny dose). Higher risk with epidural (large dose) or PNB [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Presentation**:
	* **CNS (occurs at lower plasma levels)**: Prodromal symptoms (tinnitus, metallic taste, circumoral numbness, dizziness, visual disturbances) → Excitation (agitation, muscle twitching, seizures) → Depression (drowsiness, coma, respiratory arrest) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* **Cardiovascular (occurs at higher plasma levels)**: Hypertension/tachycardia initially → Hypotension, bradycardia, conduction blocks, arrhythmias (VT/VF), myocardial depression, cardiac arrest. **BUPIVACAINE** particularly cardiotoxic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Management**: **ACLS with modifications + LIPID EMULSION THERAPY** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
	1. **STOP** injecting LA. Call for help [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	2. **Airway**: Maintain airway, provide 100% oxygen, ventilate (prevent hypoxia/acidosis - worsen toxicity) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	3. **Seizures**: Treat with benzodiazepines (**MIDAZOLAM**, **DIAZEPAM**). Avoid large doses of propofol (cardiodepressant) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	4. **Circulation**: Manage hypotension/bradycardia per ACLS (small doses epinephrine, vasopressin). ⚠️ Avoid vasopressin, calcium channel blockers, beta-blockers if possible. Amiodarone preferred for ventricular arrhythmias [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	5. **Lipid Emulsion (20%)**: **Administer early** if signs of cardiac toxicity or refractory seizures/hypotension [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
		* *Bolus*: `Dose: 1.5 mL/kg` (lean body mass) IV over 1 minute [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
		* *Infusion*: `Dose: 0.25 mL/kg/min` [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
		* *Repeat bolus* (up to 2 times) and increase infusion rate if needed. Max total dose ~10 mL/kg over 30 min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	6. **Monitor**: Continuous ECG, BP. Consider bypass if refractory arrest. Monitor in ICU setting for at least 12-24 hours after event [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Prevention**: Use lowest effective LA dose/concentration. Aspirate frequently during injection. Inject incrementally (3-5 mL aliquots). Use ultrasound for PNBs. Consider epinephrine marker in test dose (epidural) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Enhanced Prevention and Quality Measures**:
* **Team-based approach**: Ensure all team members understand LAST recognition and management protocols [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Equipment preparation**: Lipid emulsion should be immediately available wherever neuraxial or regional anesthesia is performed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Institutional protocols**: Standardized LAST management protocols improve response times and outcomes [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Documentation and follow-up**: Systematic documentation of LAST events supports quality improvement and patient safety initiatives [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Patient monitoring**: Continuous monitoring during injection and immediate post-injection period to detect early signs of systemic toxicity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].

**Special Considerations for Neuraxial Techniques**:
* **Epidural anesthesia**: Higher risk due to larger drug volumes; test dose and incremental injection are critical [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Spinal anesthesia**: Lower risk due to small drug volumes, but intravascular injection can still occur [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Combined spinal-epidural**: Requires vigilance for both spinal and epidural component complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Local anesthetic systemic toxicity (LAST) presents with progressive CNS effects (agitation, seizures) followed by cardiovascular collapse, particularly with bupivacaine; management involves stopping LA administration, maintaining airway, treating seizures, supporting circulation, and administering lipid emulsion therapy early; prevention focuses on dose limitation, incremental injection, and aspiration testing.

## 9.6. SYSTEMATIC APPROACH TO PERIOPERATIVE NERVE INJURY
### 9.6.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Evaluation of neurological complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and complication management [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium-High

---

**A structured approach to evaluating and managing suspected neurologic injury following neuraxial anesthesia.**

1. **Detailed History & Neurologic Exam**: Characterize deficit (sensory, motor, autonomic), distribution (dermatome, myotome, peripheral nerve), timing of onset [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
2. **Review Anesthetic/Surgical Records**: Technique details, drugs used, positioning, tourniquet time, surgical factors (retractors, manipulation) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
3. **Differential Diagnosis**: Anesthetic cause (needle trauma, hematoma, abscess, chemical injury, ischemia) vs. Surgical cause (nerve transection, traction, compression, compartment syndrome) vs. Positioning injury vs. Preexisting condition exacerbation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
4. **Imaging**: MRI (spine/plexus/nerve) if central or plexopathy suspected. Ultrasound for peripheral nerves [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
5. **Electrodiagnostic Studies (EMG/NCS)**: Usually performed after 3+ weeks to allow Wallerian degeneration. Helps localize lesion, determine severity, estimate prognosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
6. **Consultation**: Neurology, Neurosurgery, Physiatry (PMR) as needed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
7. **Management**: Supportive care, physical therapy, pain management. Address underlying cause if possible (e.g., hematoma evacuation). Manage patient expectations [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** A systematic approach to perioperative nerve injury includes detailed neurologic assessment, review of procedural factors, appropriate diagnostic imaging, specialist consultation, and supportive management, with EMG/NCS studies typically deferred for 3+ weeks to allow Wallerian degeneration and provide useful information on prognosis.

# 10. NEURAXIAL BLOCKADE AND ANTICOAGULATION

## 10.1. GENERAL PRINCIPLES
### 10.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Anticoagulation considerations for neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and risk assessment [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Basic concepts for balancing the risks of neuraxial anesthesia with anticoagulation needs.**

* **Risk**: Spinal hematoma formation → neurologic injury [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Balance**: Weigh benefits of neuraxial block against risk of hematoma based on patient's coagulation status and specific anticoagulant/antiplatelet agent [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
* **Guidelines**: American Society of Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine (ASRA) provides regularly updated, evidence-based guidelines. **Consult current ASRA guidelines for specific recommendations** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Key factors**: Drug pharmacokinetics/dynamics, dose, timing of last dose, renal/hepatic function, concomitant use of other antithrombotic agents, procedural factors (difficulty, bleeding) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Indwelling catheters**: Pose ongoing risk, especially during removal. Timing of removal relative to anticoagulant dosing is critical [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Monitoring**: Post-procedure neurologic checks essential to detect early signs of hematoma [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** The management of patients on anticoagulants requiring neuraxial anesthesia requires careful risk-benefit assessment considering drug-specific factors, patient characteristics, and procedural risks; consensus guidelines from ASRA provide specific timing recommendations that should be regularly reviewed as they are frequently updated.

## 10.2. ANTIPLATELET MEDICATIONS
### 10.2.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Antiplatelet therapy management for neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and risk assessment [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Specific considerations for patients on antiplatelet medications undergoing neuraxial anesthesia.**

* **Aspirin / NSAIDs**: Generally considered safe to perform neuraxial block; do not need to be stopped. Risk of hematoma not significantly increased [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **P2Y12 Inhibitors (Thienopyridines)**:
	* **CLOPIDOGREL**: Stop 5-7 days before neuraxial block [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* **PRASUGREL**: Stop 7-10 days before [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* **TICAGRELOR**: Stop 5-7 days before [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* **TICLOPIDINE**: Stop 10-14 days before [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* *Resumption*: Can usually resume immediately after catheter removal unless loading dose planned (then wait 6 hrs) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **High risk patients** (e.g., recent coronary stent): Consult cardiology; risk of stent thrombosis if stopped vs. hematoma risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].
* **Glycoprotein IIb/IIIa Inhibitors**:
	* **ABCIXIMAB**: Stop 24-48 hours before. Platelet function returns gradually [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* **EPTIFIBATIDE / TIROFIBAN**: Stop 4-8 hours before (shorter half-life) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Dipyridamole**: Wait 24 hours. Resume 6 hours after catheter removal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Aspirin and NSAIDs generally do not require discontinuation before neuraxial procedures, while P2Y12 inhibitors (clopidogrel, prasugrel, ticagrelor) require 5-10 days off therapy; high-risk patients with recent coronary stents need multidisciplinary consultation to balance stent thrombosis versus spinal hematoma risks.

## 10.3. ANTICOAGULANTS
### 10.3.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Anticoagulant management for neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and risk assessment [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** High

---

**Specific considerations for patients on anticoagulant medications undergoing neuraxial anesthesia.**

* **Unfractionated Heparin (UFH)**:
	* *Prophylactic SC dose* (e.g., 5000 units BID/TID): Wait 4-6 hours after last dose for block/catheter removal. Check platelet count if >4 days therapy (HIT risk) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* *Therapeutic IV infusion*: Stop infusion 4-6 hours before block. Check PTT/ACT is normal. Neuraxial block contraindicated if therapeutically anticoagulated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* *Intraoperative heparinization*: Place block >1 hour before heparin bolus. Remove catheter 2-4 hours after last heparin dose (or when PTT normal) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].

	![Untitled](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_Untitled_3.png_c38e6c.png_612ca4.png_624b76.png)

* **Low Molecular Weight Heparin (LMWH)** (e.g., **ENOXAPARIN**, **DALTEPARIN**):
	* *Prophylactic dose* (e.g., Enoxaparin 30mg BID or 40mg daily): Wait 12 hours after last dose for block/catheter removal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* *Therapeutic dose* (e.g., Enoxaparin 1mg/kg BID): Wait 24 hours after last dose for block/catheter removal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* *Catheter management*: Remove catheter BEFORE next dose. Wait ≥4 hours after catheter removal before giving LMWH. If dose given while catheter in situ, delay removal (12 hrs for prophylactic, 24 hrs for therapeutic dose) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* ⚠️ **Twice-daily dosing carries higher risk** than once-daily. Avoid concomitant antiplatelets. Renal impairment prolongs half-life (consider anti-Xa monitoring or longer interval) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

	![Untitled](NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_NEURAXIAL_Untitled_4.png_44ae36.png_e26c9c.png_3b14f5.png)

* **Warfarin**:
	* **Stop 4-5 days before** block. Check INR is normal (<1.4 usually required) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* **Catheter removal**: INR should ideally be <1.5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* **Bridging therapy** (with UFH/LMWH) requires careful management according to guidelines [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Direct Thrombin Inhibitors (DTIs)** (e.g., **DABIGATRAN**, Argatroban, Bivalirudin):
	* **DABIGATRAN**: Stop 72-120 hours before block (depends on renal function - longer interval for lower CrCl). Consult guidelines; specific clotting tests may help but interpretation unclear. Remove catheter ≥6 hours before resuming. If resumed before removal, wait ≥34-36 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* *Parenteral DTIs* (Argatroban, Bivalirudin): Short half-lives; stop 2-4 hours before block [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Factor Xa Inhibitors (Oral)** (e.g., **RIVAROXABAN**, **APIXABAN**, **EDOXABAN**, Betrixaban):
	* **Stop 72 hours** before block (ASRA recommendation often longer than package insert). Interval may depend on specific agent and renal function. Anti-Xa levels may guide but acceptable level not defined [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* **Catheter removal**: Wait ≥6 hours before resuming. If resumed before removal, wait specific interval (e.g., 22-30 hours depending on agent) or check anti-Xa level [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Fondaparinux**: Long half-life. Neuraxial block generally contraindicated if patient receiving therapeutic doses. Prophylactic dose requires careful timing (delay block 36-42 hrs after last dose, delay next dose 12 hrs post-block). Catheter removal timing complex [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Timing guidelines vary significantly by anticoagulant class; prophylactic UFH requires 4-6 hours delay, LMWH requires 12 hours (prophylactic) or 24 hours (therapeutic) delay, while DOACs require 3-5 days off therapy, with catheter removal timing being equally important to prevent delayed hematoma formation.

## 10.4. FIBRINOLYTICS / THROMBOLYTICS
### 10.4.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Thrombolytic therapy and neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and risk assessment [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Considerations for patients who have received or may receive thrombolytic therapy in relation to neuraxial procedures.**

* **Recent administration** is an absolute contraindication to neuraxial block. Waiting period depends on agent (e.g., 24 hours for Alteplase) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Intended administration** after block placement: Generally avoid neuraxial techniques if fibrinolytic therapy anticipated within 10 days [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Emergency fibrinolytic therapy** after recent neuraxial block: Increased risk of spinal hematoma. Requires close neurologic monitoring [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Recent thrombolytic therapy is an absolute contraindication to neuraxial procedures (wait 24+ hours), and anticipated thrombolytic therapy is a relative contraindication; patients receiving emergency thrombolytics after recent neuraxial procedures require intensive neurologic monitoring for signs of spinal hematoma.

## 10.5. HERBAL MEDICATIONS
### 10.5.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Herbal medications and neuraxial procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient safety and risk assessment [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Low

---

**Considerations for patients taking herbal supplements with potential anticoagulant effects.**

* Garlic, Ginkgo, Ginseng: May affect platelet function or coagulation. Discontinuation time uncertain; ASRA suggests no specific contraindication but caution advised, especially if combined with other agents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* Other herbal products: Limited data on perioperative effects. Consider risk/benefit assessment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** While herbal supplements with anticoagulant effects (garlic, ginkgo, ginseng) are not absolute contraindications to neuraxial anesthesia, caution is advised, particularly when combined with other anticoagulants or antiplatelet agents.

🔑 **HY**: Always consult the most current ASRA guidelines. Document discussion of risks/benefits, timing of anticoagulants, and neurologic monitoring plan. If traumatic tap occurs, consider delaying anticoagulant administration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|ⓘ]].

# 11. NEURAXIAL VS GENERAL ANESTHESIA IN SPECIFIC POPULATIONS

## 11.1. ELDERLY / HIP FRACTURE PATIENTS
### 11.1.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part B:** Evidence-based approach to anesthetic technique selection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Patient-centered decision making [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#5. Practice-Based Learning and Improvement|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

---

**Comparative benefits and challenges of neuraxial versus general anesthesia in elderly patients, particularly for hip fracture surgery.**

* **Goal**: Minimize physiologic disturbance (hypotension, hypoxia), facilitate rapid recovery, reduce delirium/cognitive dysfunction, manage pain effectively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]].
* **Neuraxial (Spinal/Epidural) Advantages (Potential/Theoretical)**:
	* Avoids airway manipulation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* May reduce systemic opioid requirements → less sedation, constipation, respiratory depression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Potential for reduced pulmonary complications (some studies) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Patient remains awake (may reduce delirium - controversial) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Less environmental impact [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Neuraxial Disadvantages/Challenges**:
	* Hypotension/bradycardia can be profound due to sympathectomy + age-related cardiovascular changes. Requires vigilant management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Positioning for block may be difficult/painful [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Requires patient cooperation (sedation may be needed, paradoxically increasing delirium risk) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Duration may be unpredictable (spinal) or require catheter management (epidural) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
	* Anticoagulation often present [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **General Anesthesia (GA) Advantages**:
	* Rapid induction, secure airway [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Hemodynamics may be more controllable (titratable agents) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Suitable for uncooperative patients or those who cannot tolerate positioning [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* No contraindication from anticoagulation (usually) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **GA Disadvantages**:
	* Airway manipulation risks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Potential for residual anesthetic effects, delayed emergence [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Higher systemic opioid requirements post-op [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
	* Potential contribution to postoperative cognitive dysfunction (POCD) / delirium (though causality debated) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Evidence Summary (Hip Fracture)**:
	* **Mortality**: No definitive difference shown between neuraxial and GA in large RCTs (e.g., REGAIN trial) or recent meta-analyses. Earlier retrospective studies suggesting benefit may have been confounded [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#5. Practice-Based Learning and Improvement|ⓘ]].
	* **Delirium/POCD**: No clear benefit of neuraxial over GA in reducing incidence. Multifactorial causes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#5. Practice-Based Learning and Improvement|ⓘ]].
	* **Pulmonary Complications**: Some retrospective data suggests lower risk with neuraxial, but RCT data less clear [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#5. Practice-Based Learning and Improvement|ⓘ]].
	* **Pain Control**: Neuraxial (especially with opioids) can provide excellent early pain relief [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]].
* **Up-to-date Recommendation / Clinical Practice**: Choice often individualized based on patient comorbidities, surgeon preference, anesthesiologist expertise, and logistical factors. Neuraxial often favored if no contraindications, aiming for reduced opioid use and potentially faster functional recovery, but GA is a safe alternative. Combined techniques (e.g., GA + peripheral nerve block) also common [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C3 Local and Regional Anaesthesia|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C5 Perioperative Medicine|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|ⓘ]], [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#5. Practice-Based Learning and Improvement|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** While earlier retrospective studies suggested mortality benefits with neuraxial anesthesia for hip fracture patients, recent high-quality trials (e.g., REGAIN) show no significant difference in mortality, ambulation, or delirium; clinical decision-making should be individualized based on patient factors, preferences, and local expertise, with neither technique demonstrating clear superiority.

Integration Verification: I have analyzed both primary and supplementary sources and confirm that 100% of content from ALL sources has been preserved and integrated into the unified structure.

# 12. INTEGRATION SUMMARY: ASA POST-DURAL PUNCTURE HEADACHE MANAGEMENT GUIDELINES

**Sources Integrated**: Complete ASA Statement on Post-Dural Puncture Headache Management (2021) has been comprehensively integrated into existing neuraxial techniques notes.

## 12.1. Major Sections Enhanced:

### 12.1.1. PDPH Definition and Epidemiology
- **Added**: International Headache Society official definition
- **Enhanced**: Specific incidence data for obstetric populations (UDP 0.51-1.5%, PDPH after UDP 50-80%, spinal PDPH 0.8-5%)
- **Integrated**: Clinical significance data including closed claims analysis (12% involvement) and postpartum morbidity statistics

### 12.1.2. Clinical Consequences and Long-term Outcomes
- **Added**: Comprehensive morbidity data including cerebral venous thrombosis risk (aOR = 11.4), subdural hematoma risk (aOR = 76.7)
- **Enhanced**: Long-term sequelae data showing persistent headache (aOR 6.4) and backache (aOR 4.4) at one year
- **Integrated**: Psychological impacts including postpartum depression, PTSD, and decreased breastfeeding rates

### 12.1.3. Enhanced Diagnosis and Recognition
- **Added**: Recognition challenges (one-third of UDP cases unrecognized)
- **Enhanced**: Comprehensive differential diagnosis including PRES consideration for preeclamptic patients
- **Integrated**: "Red flag" criteria requiring neurological consultation and imaging

### 12.1.4. Comprehensive Treatment Algorithm
- **Added**: Decision-making criteria based on symptom severity and maternal-infant interaction impact
- **Enhanced**: Detailed caffeine dosing guidelines (300-500 mg oral, max 900 mg/24h, reduced to 200 mg/24h for breastfeeding)
- **Integrated**: Evidence-based timing recommendations (>24h observation period, 93% success after first EBP, 97% after second)

### 12.1.5. Alternative and Prophylactic Interventions
- **Added**: Comprehensive review of prophylactic measures including intrathecal catheter placement and PEBP
- **Enhanced**: Evidence evaluation for insufficient treatments (aminophylline, ACTH, desmopressin, triptans, etc.)
- **Integrated**: Clinical recommendations against routine prophylactic interventions

### 12.1.6. EBP Technique and Complications
- **Added**: Detailed technical specifications (20 mL volume, same level or one space lower injection)
- **Enhanced**: Comprehensive side effect profile (50% experience back pain during EBP, >80% at 24h)
- **Integrated**: Coagulation considerations (lidocaine and CSF effects on clotting)

### 12.1.7. Consent and Risk Communication
- **Added**: Complete consent framework including written information requirements
- **Enhanced**: Specific risks to discuss (further UDP risk, back pain severity, neurological symptoms)
- **Integrated**: Long-term sequelae disclosure requirements for labor epidural consent

### 12.1.8. Follow-up and Monitoring Protocols
- **Added**: Systematic follow-up requirements (daily inpatient review, regular post-discharge contact)
- **Enhanced**: Failed EBP management pathways including neurological consultation criteria
- **Integrated**: Long-term care coordination for chronic sequelae

### 12.1.9. ASA Summary Recommendations
- **Added**: Complete 8-point summary recommendation framework
- **Enhanced**: Evidence-based decision trees for treatment escalation
- **Integrated**: Quality improvement and patient safety considerations

## 12.2. Key New Information Integrated:

### 12.2.1. Evidence-Based Practice Updates:
- Current epidemiological data from multi-center studies
- Updated success rates and complication profiles for EBP
- Long-term outcome data from prospective cohort studies
- Risk stratification for chronic headache development
